#2985. 3 Industrial Stocks Trading Above $500 a Share: Which One Splits First?

Caterpillar, Comfort Systems, and GE Vernova all have very high stock prices that a stock split would help address.

By Reuben Gregg Brewer – Nov 21, 2026 at 10:10PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Caterpillar ›

Wall Street gets excited about stock splits, with some investors attempting to get ahead of a split before it is announced. Caterpillar (CAT -5.01%), Comfort Systems (FIX -6.42%), and GE Vernova (GEV -7.53%) are all candidates for a stock split, with share prices that are, in some cases, well above $500. Even after the pullback in the artificial intelligence trade, each of these stocks remains a strong candidate for a stock split. Based on its price, Comfort Systems could be the first to do so. Here's what you need to know.

From a purely functional point of view, a stock split makes a company's stock cheaper. For example, as of this writing, Comfort Systems' stock price was roughly $1,100 per share (rounding down). A two-for-one split would lower that to around $550 per share; a 10-for-1 split would bring the price down to $110. It is clearly easier for an investor to afford to buy a single share of a $110 stock than it is to buy a single share of a $1,100 stock. That's as true on the financial side as it is one the emotional side, since buying a single share may not feel worth the effort while buying 10 feels like a more substantial investment, even if both investments would end up costing $1,100.

From a financial perspective, however, a stock split has very little meaning. All that is being done is that a single share is now split into two or some other number of shares. To keep the math simple, if a company had just one share, it would represent 100% ownership. A two-for-one split would simply mean that there were two shares, each representing 50% ownership. A 10-for-1 split would mean there were 10 shares, each representing 10% ownership. If you owned the original share, representing 100% ownership, you would own more shares and still have 100% ownership after a stock split. Nothing changed about the percentage of the company you own.

Meanwhile, per-share figures such as earnings per share are adjusted, but the base number remains the same. For example, if a company with one share earned $10, earnings per share would be $10. After a 10-for-1 split, the company's earnings per share would be $1, but its earnings would still be $10. Again, nothing material changed about the company.

In other words, a stock split isn't a particularly big deal. But investors often view such events as important and bid up the shares of companies that split their stocks. That remains true even as more brokers allow fractional purchases, which means you can invest a dollar amount rather than buy based on the number of whole shares you can afford.

Right now, the stock prices of each of these companies are falling. That reduces the likelihood of a cut, with GE Vernova and Comfort Systems both off their 52-week highs by nearly 50%, and Caterpillar down roughly 35% from its high. Stock splits often happen on the way up; they are less likely on the way down. The pullback is due to investors being less enthusiastic about the artificial intelligence (AI) trade than earlier in the year.

Caterpillar makes construction equipment for building AI data centers and the power systems that supply them. GE Vernova makes turbines that generate the energy AI needs to operate, with demand coming from utilities and, increasingly, directly from data center operators looking to generate their own power. And Comfort Systems is a building and service provider that works on mechanical, electrical, and plumbing systems, all of which are important for building an AI data center.

All three of these industrial companies have huge backlogs and will likely benefit from AI spending for years to come. That said, Comfort Systems has the highest stock price here, at a bit over $1,100. Cat is next at around $750, with GE Vernova at a bit over $530. And that's after the large drawdowns noted above. Given this information, Comfort Systems seems like the most likely candidate for a stock split.

At the end of the day, stock splits are more of an emotional phenomenon than a financial one. You probably shouldn't buy a stock just because you think it may split. You should buy it because you like the company enough to own it, preferably for the long term.

#2983. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.

#2981. Midterms 2026: Here's Exactly What I'm Doing With My Investment Portfolio Right Now

Investors don’t need to do a lot of post-election portfolio tinkering, but some ETFs may be worth examining.

By Todd Shriber – Nov 21, 2026 at 10:30PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Vanguard Energy ETF ›

Elections have a way of eliciting emotional, borderline-visceral reactions in the investment community. Still, the knee-jerk responses are often confined to the immediate aftermath and, over time, cooler heads ultimately prevail.

For those in the camp of doing nothing with their portfolios following the blue wave, there's nothing wrong with that line of thinking. That's a calm, rational approach, and one that is validated by history.

Since 1950, the S&P 500 (^GSPC -3.70%) has been positive in the year following every midterm election, with an average gain of 15% over those 12 months. So if history holds up, now isn't the time to sell stocks.

Investors don’t need to do a lot post-election portfolio tinkering, but these ETFs may be worth examining. Image source: Getty Images.

On the other hand, changes in congressional majorities may usher in opportunities for tactical investors to capitalize on the new legislative landscape. Likewise (and perhaps surprisingly), some sectors and industries previously considered vulnerable to the Democratic sweep may prove to be less so. With that in mind, here are some exchange-traded funds (ETFs) I'm spying following the election.

It's not surprising that the healthcare sector is in the spotlight following Election Day, but this is an example of investors needing to be tactical. Said another way, a standard pharmaceutical-heavy ETF may not be the best way to capitalize on the shift of power in Congress. That's why I'm evaluating the iShares U.S. Healthcare Providers ETF (IHF +0.74%).

This $1.6 billion ETF, which turned 20 years old in May, holds shares of health insurance providers and service operators. Those could be some of the best healthcare stocks to own at a time when Democrats could push to restore, even in part, the Affordable Care Act's enhanced premium subsidies that fell by the wayside at the end of 2025. There's also chatter about momentum to add long-term care coverage to Medicare, which could benefit some of this ETF's 61 holdings.

The new congressional majority could also scrap or significantly limit planned cuts to Medicaid. If Democrats are successful in leveraging government funding to increase insurance coverage, some of this ETF's holdings will benefit from higher enrollment and reduced exposure to uncompensated care.

Be advised that this ETF is top-heavy, as UnitedHealth and CVS Health combine for nearly a third of its portfolio.

The Vanguard Energy ETF (VDE -0.73%) and the Vanguard Information Technology ETF (VGT -7.00%) are both examples of sector ETFs that were decked following Election Day. Since Nov. 4, the first trading day after Election Day, the energy fund dipped 5.3% while its tech counterpart slumped 15.3%.

The reasons differ. As has been widely noted, data center stocks are retreating not only because Democrats will control Congress but also because the party holds 30 of 50 governors' mansions, including some in states where data centers were front and center on Election Day.

Clearly, rhetoric opposed to data centers is effective on the campaign trail. Still, the Vanguard tech ETF may not be worth dumping today because it's hard to get data center moratoriums across the legislative finish line. Before Election Day, only New York state was successful in climbing that mountain.

As for the Vanguard Energy ETF, its post-election weakness isn't surprising. Renewable energy stocks often rally when polls and election results favor Democrats while the fossil fuel names that dominate this Vanguard ETF languish. That scenario played out this year.

Don't rush to abandon or ignore this Vanguard fund. The reasoning is simple: The energy sector has noteworthy short-term earnings momentum, and the group is proving to be an effective portfolio hedge while high interest rates diminish the diversification benefits of bonds.

On that note, it should be acknowledged that energy stocks have been negatively correlated with the broader market for much of this year, implying that the Vanguard Energy ETF can enhance a portfolio’s diversification.

#2980. CAVA vs. Chipotle Mexican Grill: Comparing Revenue Trends Between Two Restaurant Giants

Chipotle has outearned CAVA in every quarter since late 2024, but CAVA's revenue swings are sharper.

By Robert Izquierdo – Nov 21, 2026 at 10:39PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Cava Group ›

CAVA Group (CAVA -4.67%) runs Mediterranean fasts-casual restaurants and sells food to grocery stores. It operates 500 locations, and opened its first Wisconsin restaurant, located in the city of Greenfield, on Oct. 28. It identified Madison, Wisconsin, as its next planned site for restaurant expansion in the state.

Chipotle Mexican Grill (CMG -2.07%) oversees a chain of restaurants focusing on a limited menu of burritos and bowls made from fresh ingredients. It opened its first restaurant location in Seoul, South Korea on Sept. 2. It appointed Sabir Sami to its Board of Directors on Sept. 14.

Revenue represents the total money brought in by sales before any expenses are deducted. This metric helps investors measure a company's overall size, market footprint, and long-term trajectory..

CAVA vs. Chipotle Mexican Grill Revenue over the last 8 quarters. Note: for Q1 2025, Q1 2026, the two companies' reporting periods differ in length by more than 14 days due to differing fiscal calendars, which can make the change between quarters look bigger or smaller than an apples-to-apples comparison would show.

Data source: Financial Modeling Prep. Note: for Q1 2025, Q1 2026, the two companies' reporting periods differ in length by more than 14 days due to differing fiscal calendars, which can make the change between quarters look bigger or smaller than an apples-to-apples comparison would show. Data as of Nov. 20, 2026.

Revenue trends between CAVA and Chipotle demonstrate a textbook contrast between an early-stage hyper-growth chain and a mature, massive-scale compounder. While CAVA expanded its trailing 12-month revenue more than three times faster than its restaurant rival, Chipotle generated over nine times CAVA's quarterly sales volume with greater seasonal stability.

CAVA's brisk top-line expansion is heavily fueled by aggressive store count growth. It reached 500 locations in the third quarter of 2026 on its path toward 1,000 stores by 2032. Chipotle's sales expansion also relies on new store openings, but with over 4,000 locations, it also relies on revenue growth in existing stores.

Due to CAVA's rapidly expanding revenue, its share price valuation has soared. Its forward price-to-earnings ratio exceeds 71 while Chipotle is at a far more reasonable 27. As a result, CAVA's share price heavily depends on sales growth. If that slows, its stock will fall.

Chipotle's scale allows it to be more resilient. Even when consumer spending tightens and comparable sales flatten, its annual store expansion and high digital mix, which was 38.8% of Q3 food sales, sustain high-single-digit top-line growth and substantial cash flow.

#2977. Netflix Falls Below $60. Bill Ackman Explains Why That’s an Incredible Investment Opportunity.

A lower share price in the short term could be great in the long run.

By Adam Levy – Nov 21, 2026 at 11:00PM EST

Netflix's (NFLX -2.96%) share price has been cut in half from the all-time high it reached last summer. The stock now trades below $60 per share, as of this writing.

While there are reasons for the huge drop in Netflix, including slowing revenue growth and concerns about engagement, the drop prompted billionaire investor Bill Ackman to add the stock back to Pershing Square's (PS -8.77%) portfolio earlier this year. The stock has continued to drop, though, but Ackman doesn't see that as a concern. In fact, he sees it as an incredible opportunity for investors.

To be sure, Netflix's revenue growth has slowed considerably this year. Revenue grew between 15.9% and 17.6% in the four quarters through the first quarter of this year. That rate slowed to 13.4% in the second quarter and 11.8% in the third quarter. Management forecasts just 11.5% growth in the fourth quarter.

Management wasn't afraid to touch on the reasons for the slowdown. Co-CEO Ted Sarandos said the U.S. is "just not growing as fast as it could or as fast as we'd like," during the company's third-quarter earnings call. The advertising business has also been weaker than expected. "We are currently a bit behind our aggressive target for the year," Co-CEO Greg Peters said on the call. And that stems from disappointing engagement. "We want to grow view hours faster. We're not satisfied. We're working on it," CFO Spencer Neumann said.

But investors may be overlooking a key detail about Netflix's business that hasn't changed even as revenue growth slows. Management is highly effective at dialing in content spend and reaching its target operating margin. Operating margin is on track to reach 31.5% this year, up from 29.5% last year.

What's more, Netflix's free cash flow conversion is very high and growing. It generated $9.5 billion of free cash flow through the first three quarters of the year. Netflix uses that cash to buy back shares, which supports significant improvements in its earnings per share.

That's where Ackman believes the current share price makes Netflix stock a compelling buy. "We'll make more money over time if the stock is cheaper in the short- to intermediate-term because they're going to cancel a lot more shares as a result," he said in a recent interview.

Netflix repurchased $4.8 billion of its stock in the third quarter, and it still has $22.3 billion in authorization capacity. At the company's current share price, the remaining authorization represents around 9% of the current share count.

Netflix could complete the remaining authorization over the next two years. Management expects to generate about $2.5 billion in free cash flow in the fourth quarter, and that number should steadily climb next year. The company should be able to maintain its double-digit revenue growth rate for the foreseeable future with improvements in advertising, pricing, and maintaining a low churn rate while operating margin continues to expand. That should result in higher free cash flow conversion, meaning free cash flow grows even faster than earnings.

Ackman and his team project earnings-per-share growth of 19% per year over the next three to five years, driven by Netflix's share repurchase program. The market is pricing Netflix shares at just 22 times its free cash flow, and considering that cash is going right back to shareholders through buybacks, it seems like a fantastic opportunity for investors.

#2971. Mark Zuckerberg’s Meta Platforms Is Pouring Up to $145 Billion Into AI This Year. Investors Should Watch This 1 Number.

By Neil Patel – Nov 21, 2026 at 11:11PM EST

10 stocks we like better than Meta Platforms ›

Mark Zuckerberg founded Facebook in his Harvard dorm room in 2004. It didn't take long to grow the social media platform into one of the most powerful technology enterprises on Earth. Called Meta Platforms (META -5.93%) today, it sports a market capitalization of $1.6 trillion.

That sort of value creation should force investors to always give Zuckerberg free rein over his vision and how he directs the company's financial resources and talent. This is true even though recent blunders, like the metaverse project, haven't panned out. The ”Magnificent Seven” stock is up more than 1,500% since the initial public offering (as of Nov. 17), so his actions have clearly benefited shareholders.

It's much harder now to believe that Zuckerberg is following the right strategic path. Meta will invest up to $145 billion in capital expenditures (capex) in 2026, with spending likely to be much higher next year. This has turned free cash flow negative and led to a pause in share buybacks.

In Zuckerberg's mind, it's all about artificial intelligence (AI). Nervous investors need to watch this one number to judge if all this capex is actually worth it.

Meta's entire business model rests on monetizing the attention of people who interact with its free social media services. It currently has 3.64 billion daily active users across its family of apps, a gigantic figure that somehow keeps growing. The company makes its money from advertising, which represented 98% of total revenue in Q3 2026.

The logical conclusion is that Meta's AI-related spending spree is the right move if it improves advertising metrics. So far, this appears to be the case. This is already a huge business, but the growth has been fantastic.

Ad revenue rose 25.9% year over year to $63.1 billion in the third quarter. This is just slightly below what Alphabet's (GOOGL -2.70%) (GOOG -2.59%) Google Search, which is growing at a much slower pace, generated in Q3. Digging deeper, Meta's average price per ad increased 14%, and impressions were up 10%.

The ad ecosystem operates like a flywheel, benefiting from a robust network effect. Improving algorithms drive higher user engagement. Data is collected on their interests. Consequently, this attracts advertisers that want to get in front of these people. Meta generates revenue that is reinvested into its technology stack, further reinforcing the feedback loop.

AI appears to be strengthening the overall ad ecosystem. On the engagement front, advanced AI models bolster content recommendation. For advertisers, Meta is "leveraging AI to make it easier for businesses to manage campaigns, develop creative, and act on performance opportunities," according to chief financial officer Susan Li.

The company has developed and launched other AI-related offerings. It sells AI tools to small businesses and enterprises. There is insatiable demand for excess compute, which Meta previously said it can sell at premium prices to outside customers. The popular Muse personal agent can bring in subscription sales and capture commerce revenue share as well. These are immaterial right now, however.

Advertising is the most important area that investors should watch closely. It's a signal that the hundreds of billions of dollars in capex is driving better financial performance in the core operations. And perhaps most importantly, a thriving ad segment suggests Meta's economic moat is widening.

The AI infrastructure providers get all the love from Wall Street. This includes the chipmakers, cloud platforms, and AI model developers. Their trillion-dollar valuations reflect the market's appreciation.

But maybe the ultimate winners won't be the companies creating AI. That's an idea worth considering. Most of the value could eventually accrue to businesses that leverage this impressive technology to better serve their customers and improve their lives in unique ways.

Based on how well its ad machine is working, Meta is doing exactly this.

#2969. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on the growth potential of these 3 stocks

Fears around the artificial intelligence trade and higher pressure on oil prices are weighing on global markets, but a few stocks offer investors solid growth prospects even in choppy times.

Investors with a long-term horizon can look beyond the ongoing volatility and track the recommendations of top Wall Street analysts to pick attractive stocks with strong long-term growth potential.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Health insurer UnitedHealth delivered better-than-expected results for the third quarter of 2026. The company also raised its full-year earnings outlook, reflecting continued progress on its turnaround plan.

Following the Q3 print, Morgan Stanley analyst Erin Wright reiterated a buy rating on UNH stock with a price target of $529. "UNH's beat & raise reinforces turnaround momentum, with improving MA [Medicare Advantage]/Optum Health supporting a higher '26 EPS baseline & upside to '27 consensus," said Wright.

The five-star analyst added that better-than-expected performance in the UnitedHealthcare and Optum Health businesses supported another upgrade to the company's 2026 adjusted earnings per share outlook to at least $20.50. Wright noted that UNH raised the earnings guidance despite incremental investments planned for the fourth quarter of 2026.

Furthermore, Wright highlighted management's view that the consensus 2027 EPS of $22.63 was too conservative. Reaching even the low end of the company's 13% to 16% long-term EPS growth target would imply an EPS of $23.17.

Wright raised her 2026 EPS estimate to $20.65 from $19.82 and 2027 EPS to $23.24 from $22.54, reflecting the latest upside and an improving 2026 medical loss ratio to 87.4% from 88.1%. Overall, she believes that UNH's operational performance continues to improve, increasing her confidence in its UHC strategy and Optum Health turnaround execution.

Wright ranks No. 264 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been successful 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 15%. See UnitedHealth Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Social media giant Meta Platforms has been making headlines since the launch of its Muse AI agent, which is seeing solid traction. Investors look forward to updates on the company's AI strategy when it announces its third-quarter results on Oct. 21.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Guggenheim analyst Michael Morris maintained a buy rating on Meta stock and increased his price target to $850 from $700, reflecting higher estimates for 2027. He expects Muse usage momentum and engagement levels to shape investor sentiment into the Q3 earnings call.

Specifically, Morris highlighted that, according to Apptopia, Muse reached 1.3 million daily active users in five weeks versus 300,000 in the first week. Given that Muse's current adoption represents less than 0.05% of Meta's 3.6 billion daily active user base, the analyst expects management to prioritize boosting usage over near-term monetization.

"Over time, we believe Muse monetizes primarily through businesses paying to reach consumers through their agents, consistent with the economics of Meta's core advertising business, rather than consumer wallets," said the five-star analyst. He added that the checkout integrations with Shopify and PayPal reflect this framework.

While there are concerns that a rival could overtake Muse or the recently launched Muse Wonder and push Meta into a prolonged cycle of overinvestment, Morris remains bullish due to the company's strong record in scaling WhatsApp and competing strongly with TikTok via Reels as well as free distribution to an extensive user base.

Morris ranks No. 629 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Meta Platforms KPIs on TipRanks.

Next is semiconductor company Marvell Technology. Earlier this month, Marvell held its Investor Day, where it raised its total addressable market estimate to $400 billion by 2030, reflecting solid AI-led demand tailwinds.

After hosting a non-deal roadshow with Marvell's senior management following the Investor Day, Stifel analyst Tore Svanberg reiterated a buy rating on the stock with a price target of $370.

The five-star analyst said that a major focus of the roadshow was the XPU and XPU Attach mix, as Marvell's Custom business expands toward its FY31 revenue target of $30 billion. In this regard, management reiterated that it expects a balanced mix of XPU and XPU Attach revenue in FY31, though it noted that either segment could grow rapidly enough to shift the mix toward 60/40 or 40/60.

While XPU drove most of the nearly $1.5 billion Custom revenue in FY26, Svanberg expects Marvell's XPU Attach business to grow substantially through the end of the decade, leading to a more balanced mix in this business. At the Investor Day event, the company raised its FY29 Custom target from more than $10 billion to over $12 billion, mainly driven by strength in the XPU Attach business.

Svanberg said that another key topic in the roadshow was whether Marvell can secure enough capacity to meet its raised targets. Management said that it met with suppliers ahead of Investor Day to share its five-year goals and received commitments to support these targets. The analyst believes that Marvell's supply chain commitments give it a competitive edge over rivals as customers seek reliable partners for large-scale infrastructure buildouts.

Svanberg ranks No. 91 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 31.8%. See Marvell Technology Statistics on TipRanks.

The survey is conducted for the Board by NORC, a social science research organization at the University of Chicago. Participants in the study are chosen at random from 119 geographic areas, including metropolitan areas and rural counties across the United States, using scientific sampling procedures that aim to produce a sample representative of American families.

#2965. SharonAI: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its secured-debt funding model means every GPU is backed by a customer contract, so the cash-flow test is whether those contracts convert to billing.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 21, 2026 at 11:14PM EST

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 16, 2026, SharonAI (SHAZ -18.06%) introduced an annualized run rate (ARR) revenue metric to track its commercial expansion as data center infrastructure comes online. Commercial execution now determines whether its contractual backing turns into actual cash flow. Three things management said on the call stand out.

The company's annualized run rate revenue, or projected yearly revenue based on active billing, will surge as 30 megawatts of data center power capacity enter active service. That operational activation converts initial infrastructure installations into cash-generating deployments under long-term agreements, with billing expected in January of 2027.

Rather than tapping high-cost corporate borrowing, management finances graphics processing unit (GPU) hardware using special purpose vehicles (SPVs), or isolated subsidiary companies created for specific infrastructure projects. These debt facilities lock in funding directly against customer agreements where clients must pay regardless of usage.

Customer pricing on multiyear agreements has increased as specialized compute capacity grows scarce across global markets. High contribution margins, or the proportion of revenue remaining after direct operating costs, allow project returns to pay back initial equipment costs well inside contract terms.

Customers are scrambling for immediate power and compute access, and management is not hiding its satisfaction about its secured data center footprint. The company holds pre-connected power infrastructure in Australia and an anchor partnership with NVIDIA, giving it clear advantages over rivals stuck in utility grid delay queues. Management keeps a close watch on cluster deployment timelines and debt execution.

Converting a total contract value of $9.6 billion into active billing remains the ultimate test for the business as it ramps hardware deployments into 2027.

#2957. Where Will Nvidia Stock Be in 20 Years? The Answer Might Surprise You.

Nvidia's future isn't as hard to forecast as you might think.

By Ryan Vanzo – Nov 22, 2026 at 12:00AM EST

Nvidia (NVDA -14.01%) stock is currently the cheapest it has been in over a decade. Shares trade at just 16 times trailing earnings. That's even cheaper than the company's previous decade-low valuation set in early 2019.

The company's shrinking valuation has little to do with its current financial situation. The GPU maker generated $43.8 billion in free cash flow last quarter. Sales growth this year is expected to approach 100% year over year, with another 71% growth expected the following year. Profits are expected to rise in tandem, causing shares to trade at less than 10 times forward earnings.

The macro environment for Nvidia is similarly bullish. Research from Apollo Global Management (APO -4.41%) says:

What, then, is dragging down Nvidia stock? There are three main culprits.

First, the company is dealing with the ongoing fallout of CEO Jensen Huang's affair with an alleged Chinese spy. The company could face regulatory blowback, export restrictions, and weaker long-term leadership following Huang's exit.

Second, reporting suggests that Nvidia engaged in a form of circular financing, injecting cash into artificial intelligence (AI) start-ups to absorb excess GPU supply.

Third, investors are concerned about potential margin compression as both hyperscalers and other GPU producers invest aggressively to scale their own chip production.

But here's the thing: If you commit to a 20-year holding period, few of these current concerns will ultimately matter.

Normally, it is difficult to forecast a company's next couple of decades. Nvidia's situation is surprisingly straightforward. While the company's dominant market share in AI GPUs is driven by long-term planning and software ecosystem investment, sales growth is largely driven by industrywide demand. In other words, Nvidia is a high-quality boat in a tide that will rise for decades to come.

"Nvidia has a wide economic moat," asserts research from Morningstar. "[I]ts competitive advantages should allow it to sustain profitability for 20 years," as its products and software are "needed to enable the exponentially growing market around artificial intelligence."

How quickly is that AI market growing, and how long will it sustain those growth rates?

"A massive wave of data center construction will have implications through 2050," stresses research from PwC. "The AI infrastructure cycle underway will likely dwarf previous investment booms in areas like railways, electrification, or the internet."

In no scenario does PwC forecast data center spending to fall under $20 trillion by 2050.

"Across all three scenarios, capex rises significantly through 2050," the firm predicts. "Faster AI adoption pushes capex to about $50 trillion through 2050; a slower adoption path limits the cycle to roughly $20 trillion."

While former CEO Jensen Huang was critical in developing Nvidia's current chip lineup and CUDA developer ecosystem, Nvidia's long-term future now relies just as much on industry trends as company-specific development.

Similarly, while near-term regulatory pressure or export restrictions may slightly reduce sales growth, the long-term global growth outlook remains intact.

Margin compression over time, meanwhile, is almost a certainty. But trading at less than 10 times forward earnings, much of these pressures are arguably already priced in. Compounding double-digit annual sales growth -- which should persist over the next two decades -- should more than offset margin normalization, especially considering the next few years should still generate industry-leading profitability.

What will Nvidia be like 20 years from now? The company will almost certainly be much bigger than it is today. And given how cheaply the stock is priced, I'm fairly certain that patient shareholders will be pleased following a multi-decade holding period.

The Committee decided to maintain the target range for the federal funds rate at 3-3/4 to 4 percent, in support of the Federal Reserve's dual mandate. The Committee is continuing its policy of maintaining ample reserves in the banking system.

Economic activity is expanding at a strong pace. While uncertainty remains elevated owing, in part, to geopolitical developments, domestic spending has been resilient. Voting against the monetary policy action were Lisa D. Cook and Beth M. Hammack, who preferred to raise the target range for the federal funds rate by 1/4 percentage point at this meeting.

#2954. Yelp: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Management is betting that a $250 million non-advertising revenue target can carry Yelp through a stalled local ad market before 2028.

By The Motley Fool Team – Nov 22, 2026 at 12:23AM EST

Management reduced diluted shares outstanding by 15% year over year after buying back $200 million of stock in 2026. It paused the repurchase program in the third quarter to pay down its revolving credit facility before resuming in 2027.

Management is watching key operating themes, including pricing discipline in paid project acquisition and cost control around stock-based compensation (SBC).

The broader strategy hinges on transforming search interactions into automated lead conversions across services and dining. Long-term returns depend on whether software revenue can outpace macro softness in local advertising.

#2946. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Global stock markets remain volatile amid persistent geopolitical tensions and concerns about the sustainability of AI trade. Given this backdrop, investors seeking steady income can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios.

Tracking recommendations from top Wall Street analysts can help investors pick attractive stocks, as these experts assess a company's ability to pay dividends consistently.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Integrated oil and gas giant Chevron (CVX) is a dividend king. The company has increased its dividend for 39 consecutive years. At a quarterly dividend of $1.78 per share (annualized dividend of $7.12 per share), CVX offers a yield of 3.14%. Chevron will announce its third-quarter results on October 30.

In an earnings preview note on U.S. oil majors, Goldman Sachs analyst Neil Mehta reiterated a buy rating on Chevron with a price target of $241. The analyst is focused on the company's international growth prospects across Latin America, the Middle East, West Africa, and the Eastern Mediterranean.

Specifically, Mehta highlighted management's commentary on solid execution in Venezuela, with gross production expected to more than double to nearly 600 kbd (kilo barrels per day) by 2031 via three joint ventures. He also noted a recent oil and gas condensate discovery in offshore Angola and is awaiting commercial updates on the West Qurna 2 and Nasiriyah projects in Iraq.

Furthermore, Mehta expects CVX to report improved productivity and capital efficiency across domestic shale assets. He expects the improvement mainly in the Permian Basin, where Chevron projects advanced chemical treatments to drive about a 10% recovery uplift in new wells and bring down capital spend per barrel by about 25% year-over-year. Overall, Mehta expects Chevron to report Q3 2026 EPS of $4.95, above the consensus of $4.89.

"While unfavorable timing effects and volatile commodity prices may present quarterly earnings headwinds, we highlight healthy U.S. refining margins and strong asset utilization as partial offsets," said Mehta.

Mehta ranks No. 381 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Chevron KPIs on TipRanks.

Kinetik Holdings (KNTK) is a fully integrated, pure-play, midstream company operating in the Delaware Basin. Earlier this month, the company declared a dividend of $0.81 per share, payable on October 30, 2026. With an annualized dividend of $3.24 per share, KNTK offers a yield of 5.82%. Kinetik will announce its Q3 earnings on November 4, with a conference call scheduled the next day.

Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, Goldman Sachs analyst John Mackay reiterated a buy rating on Kinetik stock and slightly raised his price target to $58 from $57. He expects the company to report Q3 2026 EBITDA of $279 million, higher than the Street's expectation of $270 million but lower than the second quarter's EBITDA of $281 million.

The 5-star analyst expects Kinetik's Q3 performance to gain from higher gas volumes after notable curtailments in the second quarter. Mackay also expects the quarter to benefit from higher production and steady commodity margins, even though some lower-margin contracts and weaker Waha spread marketing gains could weigh on results.

Overall, Mackay increased his Q3 2026 estimates to reflect higher volumes, driven by rapid Permian egress pipeline commissioning, stronger throughput at Kings Landing I, and higher M2M commodity prices, offset by increased costs.

"As natural gas takeaway constraints ease across the Permian, investor focus centers on KNTK's YE26 volume trajectory and durability of its multi-year growth," said Mackay.

Mackay ranks No. 569 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 64% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.9%. See Kinetik's Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Moving to Crescent Energy (CRGY), an exploration and production company with activities focused in the Eagle Ford, Permian, and Uinta Basins. With a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share (annualized dividend of $0.48 per share), CRGY's dividend yield stands at 3.56%. Earlier this month, Crescent announced an agreement to acquire Eagle Ford assets from Devon Energy (DVN).

Heading into Q3 earnings on Nov. 2, Evercore analyst Stephen Richardson reiterated a buy rating on Crescent stock and slightly lowered the price target to $17 from $18, citing dilution from new shares.

"CRGY's recent acquisition of DVN's Eagle Ford assets marks a strategic, growth-oriented move, potentially enhancing cash flow performance," said Richardson.

The 5-star analyst noted that the acquisition of the Eagle Ford assets at a net purchase price of $3.85 billion will add about 68 MBoe/d of net production and over 600 net Tier 1 locations. Richardson added that a solid track record of synergies from past deals in the Eagle Ford region increases confidence about Crescent achieving efficiencies with this deal.

Additionally, Richardson noted that the financial implications of the acquisition seem impressive, with a projected increase in cash flow per share of about 9% for 2027 to 2029.

The analyst believes that Crescent is not just expanding its business through acquisitions but positioning itself as a more durable company, regardless of market conditions.

Richardson ranks No. 478 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 13.2%. See Crescent Energy Statistics on TipRanks.

#2944. Anthropic raises $93.5 billion total as underwriters scoop up ‘greenshoe’ overallotment

Anthropic underwriters have officially exercised their overallotment of shares in the historic initial public offering, bringing the total raised to $93.5 billion, according to a release Friday.

The artificial intelligence company raised $81.3 billion on Wednesday and netted an additional $12.2 billion through the overallotment, surpassing SpaceX, which held a record IPO in June.

Anthropic said it sold an aggregate of 1,127,000,000 shares of its Class A common stock at $83 per share in the offering. It sold 980 million shares on Wednesday and its underwriters had the option to purchase up to an additional 147 million shares by Friday as part of the overallotment, which is commonly referred to as the "greenshoe."

Shares of the Claude maker opened trading at $111.50 on the Nasdaq on Thursday, but slid throughout the day and closed at $97.17. The stock sank 12% in premarket trading on Friday during a broad tech sell-off.

In addition to the sale of its Class A common stock, Anthropic closed a concurrent sale of its Class N common stock after a group of tech companies, including Advanced Micro Devices, Google, Microsoft, Nvidia and others, agreed to participate in a $26 billion private placement at the IPO price. Anthropic said Friday that aggregate gross proceeds from its IPO, including the private placement, totaled $119.5 billion, before deducting offering expenses.

Anthropic dodged an unforgiving series of obstacles on its road to the Nasdaq on Thursday, as investors brooded over Jensen Huang's shocking ouster at Nvidia, voters' stinging rebuke of the tech during the midterms and whether rising interest rates would stymie industry plans for a multitrillion-dollar AI infrastructure buildout.

CEO Dario Amodei co-founded the company in 2021 after he and a handful of other researchers, including his sister Daniela, quit their jobs at OpenAI, now Anthropic's chief rival. Anthropic is best known for developing a family of AI models called Claude, which have captivated the corporate world and fueled the company's historic rise.

The siblings celebrated the IPO as a "milestone" in an interview with CNBC on Thursday, but said there's more work ahead for Anthropic.

"For us, it's like one stop on the journey," Daniela Amodei said. "We think about this as, 'Wow, this is an incredible moment for Anthropic for our mission, but we're going to wake up tomorrow and we're going to be the same company.'"

#2938. Trump says he discussed Jensen Huang with Xi; Bessent tells CNBC Nvidia chip export policy is unchanged

President Donald Trump on Friday said he and Chinese leader Xi Jinping discussed their shared affinity for former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang during their talks in China this week. Earlier in the day, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent said the ousted leader was not mentioned in separate talks he had with Chinese officials.

"I did discuss that with President Xi, and he said, 'Oh, brilliant, brilliant! He's a brilliant man.' He is," Trump said at a White House press conference.

Asked earlier Friday on CNBC's "Squawk on the Street" if Huang, a close ally of Trump's, had come up in "any of your discussions with the Chinese," Bessent said "no," without elaborating.

A senior Trump administration official later said that both Trump's and Bessent's remarks were correct, and that both were addressing their own conversations. The official spoke on condition of anonymity to discuss those conversations.

Trump also reiterated that he would like to see Huang — who was replaced by interim CEO Colette Kress after resigning amid allegations of an extramarital affair with a Chinese spy — be restored as Nvidia's CEO.

"I would love to see Jensen be given a second chance," Trump said. "You know, we're a country of second chances, and I'd love to be able to see him. That's a very important company to us, and he put us in a position where we are literally 10 years advanced over other companies."

Nvidia's board is not considering bringing Huang back, a person familiar with the matter told CNBC earlier this week.

The president's comments came amid growing scrutiny of advanced U.S. chips, including those made by Nvidia, ending up in China either legally or by being diverted via third countries.

Both Trump's and Bessent's remarks are potentially an indication that the Trump administration may not focus on cracking down on China's efforts to acquire advanced chips.

Bessent suggested that U.S. policy allowing the sale of Nvidia's powerful H200 AI chips to China remains unchanged following the talks in Shenzhen this week.

"We've already agreed that the ... H200s are the threshold level of chips that could be sold to the Chinese, and, you know, that continues as planned," Bessent said.

"I wouldn't anticipate any change in the policy," he added after CNBC's Sara Eisen noted that Nvidia said it had stopped selling H200s to China.

The administration ignoring the subject of Huang's affair with a possible Chinese spy — whose Singapore-based company Megaspeed has been accused of acting as a cutout for Beijing — contrasts with the importance Trump has placed on beating China in the race to artificial intelligence dominance. It could suggest that despite his support for the U.S. AI industry, Trump is less concerned with addressing China's ongoing efforts to acquire U.S. semiconductors.

Trump has also defended Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has come under fire from multiple directions for reportedly slow-walking export restrictions on Chinese chip sales. "I understand both sides of it," Trump said on Monday when he also said he's like to see Huang back as CEO.

Bessent's appearance on CNBC came after Trump's return from a tour through Asia that saw him meet with Xi and Russian President Vladimir Putin in Shenzhen, China.

The meeting was historic as the first time the leaders of the three countries had met without other nations' counterparts. But the trilateral summit appeared to yield few deliverables on any number of the most pressing global issues related to the countries, including the Ukraine and Iran wars or the ongoing AI race.

Bessent told CNBC on Friday that there was a "robust discussion" between Trump and Xi on trade policy and AI.

They "agreed that humans should always be involved in any military decisions that SI makes, and the SI should not be allowed to be autonomous for major military actions," he said, abbreviating the term "super intelligence," Trump's attempted rebrand of AI.

In a CNBC appearance last week, Bessent lamented the "leakage" of U.S.-made AI chips to China and blamed the Commerce Department, led by Lutnick, for slow-walking export controls on suspected Chinese cutouts like Megaspeed.

Multiple Trump administration leaders have long sought to impose export restrictions on Megaspeed, but Commerce "has the pen" and "you will have to ask them about their internal processes," Bessent said in that interview.

On Friday, however, Bessent was much more tight-lipped.

Asked if there is a dispute between him and Lutnick over how aggressively the U.S. should be pursuing Chinese export controls, Bessent said, "I think we're all committed to following President Trump's instructions."

"I think we are going to see that the ... proper chip restrictions are in place, and the remote access has been curtailed," he added. Commerce recently took steps to close one of China's paths to accessing AI processors.

Correction: Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent spoke to CNBC's "Squawk on the Street." A previous version of this story misstated the name of the show.

Editor's note: This story and headline have been updated following additional information provided by the Trump administration about the nature of Bessent's and Trump's meetings in China.

#2937. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2935. Warsh wants Fed officials to talk less. Instead, they’re talking more

In his first six months as Federal Reserve chairman, Kevin Warsh has diligently avoided signaling where he sees monetary policy and interest rates headed. His colleagues have been only too happy to fill the void.

Fed officials have been rushing to the microphones lately, voicing sometimes conflicting opinions on how the central bank should be handling an inflation bug that the economy can't seem to shake.

Most agree that rates should be raised — and soon. Some are willing to hike at a measured pace as conditions play out, while others, such as Governor Christopher Waller and St. Louis Fed President Alberto Musalem, entertain the thought of more aggressive hikes in a shorter period of time.

Largely on the sidelines in the debate is Warsh, who has expressed disdain for that sort of forward guidance and argued that it handcuffs policymakers long before they actually have to make a decision. But that hasn't stopped most of the other 18 officials who sit in on Federal Open Market Committee meetings.

In fact, according to a Bank of America analysis, there has been more so-called Fedspeak in the past two weeks than in any similar period since the start of 2025.

"Warsh's refusal to guide markets might be encouraging the rest of the FOMC to speak more, not less," Bank of America economist Aditya Bhave said in a note Thursday.

"There is always a range of views across the 19 FOMC participants. Typically, the Chair would weigh in, indicating to markets whether the hawks or doves are in ascendence," he added. "Absent this leadership, however, both sides might feel incentivized to speak more often and more forcefully."

The increasingly vocal debate comes at a critical time for the Fed and has myriad policy implications.

Bhave noted "a deepening divide" on the FOMC. The Fed's decision not to increase its benchmark rate at its October meeting "increasingly looks like a mistake," and as a result "the hawks have been very vocal of late," he wrote. Leading the charge is Waller, who has raised the possibility of hiking by half a percentage point when the committee next meets in December.

Minutes from the October meeting hint at the tension, particularly when it comes to Warsh's refusal to provide guidance.

The summary related that some officials who were looking for a hike in October "could have supported no change in the federal funds rate provided that it was clearly understood by market participants that further tightening was likely" — a statement that points toward the forward guidance that Warsh has insisted the Fed avoid.

The minutes "revealed that some officials favoring a hike would have accepted a hold if the Fed had clearly communicated further tightening," market veteran Ed Yardeni wrote Thursday. "Warsh's communication philosophy may thus be limiting the Fed's flexibility precisely when it needs it most."

Cleveland Fed President Beth Hammack, who voted against the October pause in favor of a quarter-point hike, tiptoed around the forward guidance issue during a recent public appearance.

Prefacing some observations she made about where she sees policy headed, Hammack noted that, "Those are not promises. Those are not commitments. They're not forward guidance, which is a hot-button topic. But what they are are a conditional set of expectations of where I think the economy can go, based on what I'm seeing."

Hammack's comments illustrate a needle that Fed officials have been trying to thread: continuing to be transparent about their thinking without appearing locked into a future policy that changing economic conditions could render obsolete.

Speaking Thursday, Chicago Fed President Austan Goolsbee framed the argument around credibility.

"I am sympathetic to the view that [Warsh] has expressed that let's not be so, in in our language, forward guidance-oriented, saying here is what I think rates are going to be at the next meeting," Goolsbee said, calling such pronouncements "falsely precise."

However, he quickly separated forward guidance from transparency.

"I think central banks should be careful about doing anything that undermines credibility," Goolsbee said. "That said, I also think that it's pretty important [that] each person who sits around that table explain how they see the world. What do you see in business conditions? You don't want to surprise everybody."

Taking a slightly different tack, Philadelphia Fed President Anna Paulson, in a speech Thursday on policy communication, insisted that markets can figure out where policymakers are going without specific guidance so long as they understand how economic developments will influence decisions.

Paulson also defended the variety of voices coming from the Fed.

"But volume is not the same as clarity," she said. "The measure is not how much we say. It is whether we are understood." The mixture of voices "guards against any single communication becoming the only one that matters."

Financial markets have taken the plethora of Fed communications and interpreted it in a fairly hawkish way.

Traders are implying a 100% chance of at least a quarter percentage point hike in December, with a very small probability of a half-point move, according to the CME Group's FedWatch. From there, odds are high for a follow-up increase in January, then perhaps one more before the end of 2027.

While current inflation indicators are running above 3%, compared to the Fed's 2% target, market measures of longer-term inflation have declined.

Five-year forward expectations, as indicated through the Treasury market, are at their lowest since April. That suggests investors could be less concerned about inflation over the longer term, even as policymakers warn that current price pressures remain elevated.

Still, a sclerotic communications strategy from the Fed amid volatile economic and geopolitical currents could prove dangerous ahead.

"The result of this back-and-forth is undue rates volatility in already turbulent times," BofA's Bhave said.

For his part, Warsh has been advocating for a "family fight" atmosphere inside the Fed, arguing that competing viewpoints deserve a full airing as the committee works towards decisions.

"There are benefits to shaping the market's future expectations. Perhaps this is part of the 'family fight' that Warsh keeps referencing in his pressers," said Tom Graff, chief investment officer at advisory firm Facet. "That family fight seems to be happening out in public."

#2934. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guidance; Extension of Comment Period

Federal Register notice: Proposed Third-Party Risk Management Guide for Traditional Community Banking Organizations

The demand for large-scale data centers in the U.S. this year, driven by the need for computing power for artificial intelligence, grew well beyond the supply of powered land and the labor to build these structures. It also pushed timelines and risk for contractors.

All that resulted in costs rising 13% year-over-year to more than twice the global rate, according to a new report from Turner & Townsend, a global project management company. The gain this year was about three times the last ten-year average.

The report looked at over 500 live or recently completed projects in 62 global markets. It also included a survey of 400 experts in the global data center industry.

While there was some stabilization in cost inflation this year, it was not enough to offset the huge growth in not just demand but speed of construction and delivery.

"More aggressive schedules tie to contractors in particular and subcontractors baking in more risk allowances in their pricing," said Paul Barry, North America data center lead for Turner & Townsend. "So whether that's acceleration allowances, whether that's more overtime and labor attraction allowances, even just higher fees for taking on the risk of delivering at a faster pace than what they would traditionally take on."

All of those things, combined with the spike in demand and the strain on the supply chain, translate into higher costs, he added.

Capital expenditure on global data centers is projected to rise from about $800 billion this year to $1.8 trillion per year in 2050, according to a recent report from PwC. The U.S. is expected to capture almost half of all global investment in AI infrastructure, which includes both data centers and chips.

Power is, of course, key to all data centers, and 56% of those surveyed by Turner & Townsend globally said it was a major obstacle to delivery. Just over half said utility connections typically take between one and two years, and almost a third reported timeframes of over two years. Costs for power vary regionally — from $15.01 per watt in Silicon Valley to $10.51 per watt in Dallas, based on a typical 60-megawatt design.As with every type of construction in the U.S., labor continues to be a significant headwind. Just over one quarter of respondents said their contractor capacity, that is, the ability to manage construction backlogs, was sufficient.

Going forward, Barry said he expects costs to moderate somewhat, with cost increases in the 6% to 10% range in the next year. A lot of the major projects have already started or are starting now, so cost risk has been baked into those contracts.

"If this demand continues, then there'll be more continued flow of project starts and so on, but I think we've hit a big surge in 2026, especially of new big major projects that have been procured," said Barry.

#2932. Robinhood shares fall as crypto slump weighs on profits, offsetting gains in new businesses

Robinhood shares were down around 8% in pre-market trading on Wednesday as investors digested weaker-than-expected third-quarter earnings despite growth in options trading and prediction markets.

Here's how the brokerage performed in its quarter ended Sept. 30, compared with Wall Street estimates, based on a survey of analysts by LSEG:

Robinhood's net income fell to $468 million, or 51 cents per share, from $556 million, or 61 cents per share, a year ago. Revenue represented a 12% increase from $1.27 billion in the same quarter last year.

Despite that gain, transaction-based revenue of $867 million came in below Wall Street estimates from StreetAccount of $879 million, putting focus on whether Robinhood can sustain momentum across its trading businesses and reduce its reliance on volatile market activity.

Robinhood has been diversifying its business as it tries to expand its brokerage app into a broader financial services platform. Options trading and event contracts have become increasingly important sources of transaction revenue, while the company has continued to invest in wealth management, subscriptions and other offerings.

Transaction-based revenue grew 19% from the same period last year to $867 million, driven primarily by a more than 500% increase in event contracts revenue. Additionally, options revenue grew 31% to $399 million and equities revenue rose 41% to $121 million.

Those gains were partially offset by cryptocurrency revenue, which fell 55% to $120 million.

Chief Financial Officer Shiv Verma told CNBC event contracts on crypto movements did well in the quarter, offering investors another way to express a view on crypto despite the slump in prices and spot trading.

Net deposits on the Robinhood platform totaled $15.7 billion for the quarter, equal to a 17% annualized growth rate based on total platform assets at the end of Q2 2026. Over the past year, net deposits totaled $71 billion, representing 21% growth compared with total platform assets at the end of Q3 2025.

—With reporting from CNBC's Robert Hum and Kate Rooney

#2931. Walker Kessler's first three-pointer as a Laker sparks resurgent second half

Lakers coaches didn't know why Walker Kessler was standing in the corner. But when Collin Sexton flung the ball to the center standing behind the three-point arc on the Lakers' first possession of the fourth quarter, Kessler knew what he was going to do with it.

"I'm confident in that shot," Kessler said with a smirk.

The 7-foot-2 center's strong second half sparked the Lakers' gritty 106-104 win over the Sacramento Kings on Friday at Crypto.com Arena. After Lakers coach JJ Redick "lit [him] up" at halftime, Kessler responded by scoring nine of his 15 points and grabbing six of his seven total rebounds after the break and draining the unexpected long-distance shot that put the Lakers up by one early in the fourth quarter.

Redick said the play, with the Lakers (14-3) trailing by two to start the fourth quarter, was a little unorganized, leading to their center somehow standing in the corner. Kessler, who hadn't attempted a three all season and was 17 for 64 from three-point range during four seasons with the Utah Jazz, took instruction from Sexton to space to the corner instead of slash to the rim. Sexton whipped a one-handed pass over his head to Kessler, who methodically rose up over Dylan Cardwell's late closeout.

Redick told Kessler once during the preseason after a particular timeout that if the ball found him on a called play and it wasn't for a layup, the center should still shoot. Although Kessler has faith from the coaches and works on his jump shot, Redick isn't anointing the 25-year-old an official stretch five. His teammates don't mind.

"I mean, if he shoots it like that, I'll keep finding him," guard Quentin Grimes said with a laugh. "If he's going to keep cashing it all net, I want more of them. Keep shooting."

Read more: Quentin Grimes' late three-pointer lifts Lakers to narrow win over Kings

Kessler's go-ahead three-pointer highlighted the Lakers' comeback from an 11-point deficit with 11:25 remaining in the third. During a homestand that appeared to be favorable but has turned into a difficult slog, the Lakers have relied on unexpected characters to star in big moments.

Grimes hit back-to-back three-pointers in the fourth quarter against the Charlotte Hornets on Tuesday to fuel his career-high 47 points. The Lakers staged most of their 18-point comeback during the beginning of the fourth quarter when superstar Luka Doncic was on the bench. They trailed by 13 at the end of the third quarter. When Doncic subbed back in with 6:54 remaining, the Lakers, playing the majority of the stretch with two-way center Carson Cooper, were down by only four.

Cooper was playing the critical minutes because Kessler missed the game with lower right leg soreness. With Kessler back against the Kings (1-14), the Lakers repeated their fourth-quarter heroics, turning a two-point deficit after three quarters into a seven-point lead by the time Doncic returned.

Coaches made small rotational changes to the unit Friday, opting to start the fourth with Kessler, Sexton, Grimes, Matisse Thybulle and Jake LaRavia. Forward Sandro Mamukelashvili previously started the fourth quarter in recent games with Austin Reaves sidelined. Still feeling out the best lineups for the team, Redick said the exact groupings are "going to be a process."

The Lakers aren't fazed by the adjustments.

"I think we have a lot of heart and our ability to just make things work," said Thybulle, who had 14 points and four made three-pointers Friday. "We've been throwing out a whole bunch of different lineups. … Just being able to be adaptable, I think, has been a huge thing for everyone on the roster."

Lakers center Walker Kessler scores off an offensive rebound in the first half against the Sacramento Kings on Friday. (Gina Ferazzi / Los Angeles Times)

The Lakers are 6-1 in the last seven games in which Doncic or Reaves have missed time. The superstars have only played three quarters of a game together in the last seven games. Doncic missed the game at Detroit with lower back soreness, and Reaves, who was sick in Atlanta on Nov. 9, has missed the last four games with a left heel bruise. The Lakers hope to have him back by at least next Friday on the road against San Antonio.

Kessler was sidelined against Charlotte for the first time this season. Like the rest of his teammates, he struggled against the Utah Jazz on Wednesday, finishing with the quietest 10-point, 11-rebound double-double imaginable. He was in danger of fading into the background again against Sacramento before Redick jolted the team awake at halftime.

"I wasn't playing physical," Kessler said. "I wasn't going up to dunk the ball. I was missing some more touches around the rim. And then defensively, being undisciplined. Jumping at stuff I don't need to jump at. He definitely lit into me. It was good. I needed it. That's what makes a good coach. Knowing when guys need that and when guys don't."

While Redick said afterward he believed he was able to regulate his emotions during his halftime talk to the team, Doncic, who finished with 25 points and eight assists, characterized it more plainly.

"He yells," Doncic said. "But I'm used to it."

Kessler, a centerpiece for a franchise with championship expectations, is getting used to the demands of this new spotlight. He dazzled with 48 points in his first two games in which he made 22 of 26 shots, including 10 dunks in his perfect debut. But coaches demanded more production from his rebounding and fans are unrelenting in their calls for more.

"I know obviously you're on a bigger stage and stuff like that, but my teammates and coaching staff have done a really good job of picking everyone up even though we're struggling or we were struggling these past two games," Kessler said. "I'm not really focused too much on the outside but I understand that the L.A. fans expect a certain kind of performance. And I think it's not just making shots, but playing with effort and energy and I can completely respect that."

When Kessler goes to the bench, he hears jeers from fans imploring him to play harder.

When he hit that three from the corner, he heard some of the loudest cheers of the night.

#2926. Trump vows to stop U.S. companies from building data centers abroad, threatens 'tremendous tax'

President Donald Trump on Friday said U.S. companies will be banned from building data centers in other countries and will face a "tremendous tax" penalty if they do so.

"We're going to fight very hard for the data centers," Trump said at a White House press conference before announcing the new policy — which apparently came as news to his own staff.

"I don't know if that's a breaking story," Trump said. "I haven't even said that to my people. But I'm telling them now they're here."

Trump's latest push for more data-center construction in the U.S. came despite Americans repeatedly stating — and showing with their votes — that they dislike the structures.

CNBC's All-America Economic Survey found that 64% of registered voters opposed an AI data center within 10 miles of their home, while just 13% of respondents said they would support one.

If Trump follows through on his threat to clamp down on overseas data center construction, he will have to contend with the fact that numerous U.S. tech giants — including Alphabet, Amazon, Microsoft, Meta and Oracle — have already built them in other countries.

International expansion represents a growth opportunity for the top cloud infrastructure providers, which want to serve clients from data centers that are nearby, sometimes to meet regulatory requirements.

Trump said Friday, however, that if companies want to continue building the centers outside the U.S., "they can, but we're going to put a tremendous tax on them."

"We have a lot of places that want them badly, and they're great places. They have energy, and they have everything. They're tremendous assets," Trump said.

Representatives at cloud providers Amazon, Google, Microsoft and Oracle did not immediately respond to requests for comment.

Trump's remarks came a day after Trump met with Chinese President Xi Jinping and Russian President Vladimir Putin in China for a trilateral summit that appeared to produce few concrete deliverables on an array of pressing global issues.

Xi "congratulated me on how well we're doing" in artificial intelligence, Trump said, using his preferred term "super intelligence," which he said the Chinese president also likes better.

— CNBC's Jordan Novet and Annie Palmer contributed reporting.
