#2920. AI is changing software engineering — and some developers miss the struggle

When Gemma Rodgers began her computer science degree in 2002, software engineering was seen as a future-proof and lucrative career. It appealed to people who enjoyed getting into the weeds of code, tracking down bugs and building programs from scratch.

It's an industry that has always moved fast, Rodgers, a software developer based in the U.K., told CNBC. But the arrival of artificial intelligence has accelerated the pace, with AI tools now part of the daily work of every software engineer she knows. In a 2026 Stack Overflow survey, 83% of software developer respondents said they use at least one AI tool.

As AI coding tools help engineers write, debug and test code faster than ever, some are mourning the job they used to have. Rodgers has always loved the sense of accomplishment that comes from piecing together different parts of code to find a solution, particularly when she's spent days, or even weeks, trying to fix a stubborn bug.

"The sense of joy and fun that I used to experience by having a challenge and then having to work that out, it's been removed somewhat," she said. "Now you can sometimes do it in a few prompts, and it's great — but it's a breadcrumb of joy, not a full meal of joy."

Rodgers is not alone in feeling nostalgic about the not-so-old days. Software engineers have taken to social media to describe missing their old way of working, when they could get into a flow state with code for hours at a time.

Still, AI tools can be useful and have lowered the barrier to entry significantly. "It's becoming an industry where a lot of people have been able to thrive who wouldn't necessarily have been able to thrive 10 years ago, five years ago, even three years ago," Rodgers said.

It's also important that the core skills engineers have traditionally learned on the job are not lost, she said. AI tools and internet services can go down unexpectedly, and engineers still need to know how to investigate problems and work through solutions themselves.

"The sorts of skills that we were taught years ago are kind of being lost because [engineers are] having to just churn work out," Rodgers added.

The job itself is changing, too. James Broadhead, chief technology officer of AI decision-making company Taktile, told CNBC that engineers who might previously have spent three weeks building a prototype can now generate 10 in an afternoon. The hardest part of that, he said, is "having the judgment to decide which of those are any good."

"The [AI] agents are spitting out so much code that [engineers] haven't read it all anymore," Broadhead said. "It used to be you'd build up a sense of comfort and awareness of how the whole code base was structured, but now the whole thing changes all the time."

Joshua Wöhle, CEO of London-based AI training company Mindstone, told CNBC he sees a growing divide between workers who know how to use AI effectively and those who don't.

The industry used to talk about "10X engineers," he said, who were dramatically more productive than their peers. Now, AI is widening that gap even further to produce "100X engineers."

Wöhle believes companies will increasingly have to decide whether to help employees catch up with AI or prioritize those who can get the most out of it. But he also understands why some engineers might struggle to adapt.

The people who once excelled at the job were often those who loved writing and working through code, he said, and those are not necessarily the current top performers.

"That's the grief that is very, very real," Wöhle said. "The thing you love doing is no longer the thing the company should be paying for, to a degree, because it's no longer adding the value to the company that it used to."

But not every engineer sees the move away from hands-on coding as a bad thing. Chris Sees, a software engineer of more than 20 years and CEO of London-based virtual office company Hoxton Mix, told CNBC that AI gives developers more time to tackle interesting problems. He views AI as a capable junior engineer that still needs supervision.

"The important thing is knowing what to delegate and when human judgement is needed," Sees said. "We'd be silly to think AI gets everything right, and I know I get a lot of satisfaction out of proving and spotting where it has gone wrong."

Even so, Sees worries about what happens to the next generation of engineers if AI takes over the routine coding and debugging through which they traditionally learned. Those tasks are "where you learn lessons and build scar tissue," he said, and companies need to make sure AI becomes part of the learning process, rather than a shortcut.

For Rodgers, what matters is having control over how she uses the technology. As a self-employed developer, she can use AI to automate the parts of a project she finds tedious, while keeping the more interesting problem-solving for herself.

She believes there's something important about working through a difficult problem rather than immediately outsourcing it to technology. Friction and discomfort, she said, are vital parts of the human experience.

"As humans, we need to feel accomplished in something and part of accomplishment requires challenge," she said. "Unless we are able to work that through in so many different aspects of life, then you never get the full force of the good feeling on the other side."

#2917. Davis, Dybantsa lead Wizards to second straight win

The Washington Wizards rode outstanding games from Anthony Davis and AJ Dybantsa to escape Chicago with a second straight victory.

For a second straight game, the win wasn't easy, pretty, or satisfying. But it was a win. Davis dominated inside against a Bulls roster lacking a serious NBA big man. He finished with 36 points and 11 rebounds.

For my money, the most heartening thing from last night's contest was the performance of Dybantsa, who notched 20 points, 10 rebounds, and 6 assists. Reminder: he's still just 19 years old. His first step, vertical burst, and power seem surprising even in the ultra-athletic NBA — even to his ultra-athletic NBA competitors.

It's a good thing Davis and Dybantsa were great because the rest of the team didn't help much. Trae Young was okay — 16 points, 5 assists, 3 steals, and just 1 turnover. He shot 4-8 from three-point range…and 0-5 from two.

Bilal Coulibaly contributed next to nothing on offense but harassed Chicago's perimeter players on the defensive end and came up with two steals and two blocks. One of those blocks was great — with just over three minutes to go in the fourth quarter, Bulls wing Norman Powell drove. Coulibaly moved his feet, stayed with Powell step for step, stayed between Powell and the basket, and then maintained verticality when Powell pulled up. Powell ended up shooting the ball into Coulibaly's raised (and vertical) hand.

Once again, the Wizards went with a short postseason-like rotation. Head coach Brian Keefe used just eight players for more than 10 minutes, and Deandre Ayton got just 11 minutes total. Will Riley, Bub Carrington, and Tre Johnson made cameo appeareances — 6, 6, and 4 minutes respectively.

To me, it seems that Keefe trusts only a handful of players — Young, Davis, Dybantsa, and Kyshawn George. He wants to trust Alex Sarr, and Bilal Coulibaly. He's using other guys, like Khris Middleton, Ayton, Tre Johnson, Will Riley and Bub Carrington because he has to play someone.

The Bulls pregame intro video is great. It depicts charging bulls stampeding into the stadium. At one point, the herd turns towards a bus with a Wizards logo on it. A couple "players" descending the bus steps, spot the herd and flee in a panic. It amused me.

One other shoutout to the Bulls game operations crew — thank you for having a real halftime act. Last night, it was Christian and Scooby, which is an act that has not gotten old yet.

The Wizards had a terrible first possession of the game. They ran a bunch of actions to end up with George attempting a fading, drifting, contested three from the corner. Their offensive effectiveness didn't get much better. They managed an offensive rating (points scored per 100 possessions) of 101. League average this season is 114.4.

They won despite a poor offensive game because Chicago's offensive performance was somehow even worse.

The negatives keep piling up for George. Last night, he shot 5-17 from the floor and 1-8 from three-point range, and added three turnovers and five fouls. At least two of the fouls were of the dumb and pointless variety. Just hacking or grabbing an opponent for no discernible reason. Another was an offensive foul (shoving Isaac Okoro out of his way with a forearm) that might be the easiest call the refs make this season.

A play midway through the second quarter reminded me how good NBA players are. Zach Collins, a backup big man on one of the NBA's worst teams drove to the basket and spun into a lefty hook over Davis. It was an impressive display of skill, athleticism, and coordination from an NBA afterthought.

Much as I enjoy watching Dybantsa play, he can get sped up by strong defensive pressure. Several possessions per game have gotten frantic and messy. I'm looking forward to seeing those possessions cleaned up as he gains experience.

Around 8:24 in the fourth quarter, Dybantsa swooped in for an eye-popping reverse dunk in transition.

Maybe 20 seconds earlier, Coulibaly picked Powell's dribble and took the ball the other way for a breakaway dunk.

The Wizards were down eight at the half, and then blasted the Bulls, 23-9, in the third quarter and nursed the leae the rest of the ways.

4:32 — AD postup into a stepback — miss — bad shot

3:20 — impressive defense by BC — moves feet, good position, goes vertical and Powell shoots ball into BC's hand for a block

Below are the four factors that decide wins and losses in basketball — shooting (efg), rebounding (offensive rebounds), ball handling (turnovers), fouling (free throws made).

eFG% (effective field goal percentage, which accounts for the three-point shot)

TOV% (turnover percentage — turnovers divided by possessions)

FTM/FGA (free throws made divided by field goal attempts)

can get weird. In PPA, 100 is average, higher is better and replacement level is 45. For a single game, replacement level isn't much use, and I reiterate the caution about small samples sometimes producing weird results.

POSS is the number of possessions each player was on the floor in this game.

ORTG = offensive rating, which is points produced per individual possessions x 100. League average so far this season is listed in the Four Factors table above. Points produced is not the same as points scored. It includes the value of assists and offensive rebounds, as well as sharing credit when receiving an assist.

USG = offensive usage rate. Average is 20%. Median last season was 17.7%.

ORTG and USG are versions of stats created by former Wizards assistant coach Dean Oliver and modified by me. ORTG is an efficiency measure that accounts for the value of shooting, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers. USG includes shooting from the floor and free throw line, offensive rebounds, assists and turnovers.

+PTS = "Plus Points" is a measure of the points gained or lost by each player based on their efficiency in this game compared to league average efficiency on the same number of possessions. A player with an offensive rating (points produced per possession x 100) of 100 who uses 20 possessions would produce 20 points. If the league average efficiency is 115, the league — on average — would produced 23.0 points in the same 20 possessions. So, the player in this hypothetical would have a +PTS score of -3.0.

Players are sorted by total production in the game.

The University of California, Los Angeles won the 23rd Annual National College Fed Challenge.

The University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA) won the 23rd annual national College Fed Challenge on Friday, a team competition that encourages undergraduate students to learn about the U.S. economy, monetary policymaking, and the role of the Federal Reserve System. Teams analyze economic and financial conditions and formulate a monetary policy recommendation, modeling the Federal Open Market Committee. The team, from Los Angeles, California, represented the San Francisco District and included Aarushi Maheshwari, Annika Chan, Atul Prasanth, Caitlyn Cox, Finn Karish, Eli Brooks (alternate), Kennedy Yang (alternate), Manasi Vartak (alternate), Ronojoy Borpujari (alternate), and Sahil Sudani (alternate). The team's advisers were Chris Surro and Ali Haider Ismail.

"I am very happy to congratulate the students who competed in this year's College Fed Challenge, especially the winners and finalists," said Federal Reserve Chairman Kevin Warsh. "They took on the same big questions we policymakers face. I'm impressed by their talent, their rigor, and their eagerness to think hard about monetary policy and the economy."

This year's College Fed Challenge included 141 schools from 34 states across the nation, the most in the competition's history. Teams submitted video presentations or participated in local competitions last month. Twenty-one semi-finalist teams then participated in question-and-answer sessions held earlier this month, and seven finalists were invited to the Federal Reserve Board for the national finals. The other national finalists were the University of Chicago in second place and the University of Maryland, College Park in third place. Teams with honorable mentions include Cleveland State University, Harvard College, Pennsylvania State University, and St. Lawrence University.

Teams were evaluated on their economic analysis, responses to the judges' questions, teamwork, and presentations.

For media inquiries, please email [email protected] or call 202-452-2955.

Board of Governors of the Federal Reserve System

20th Street and Constitution Avenue N.W., Washington, DC 20551

#2912. Top Wall Street analysts are bullish on these 3 stocks for the long haul

The ongoing earnings season, Middle East tensions and buzz ahead of the U.S. midterm elections have been keeping investors busy. Further, worries about elevated artificial intelligence spending are weighing on investor sentiment.

Investors looking beyond the short-term noise and focusing on long-term returns can track top Wall Street analysts to pick the right stocks, backed by strong research.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

This week's first stock pick is Datadog, an AI-powered observability and security platform. The company will announce its results for the third quarter on Nov. 4. Datadog is seeing strong demand for its offerings, thanks to cloud migration and rapid AI adoption. TipRanks' KPIs tool for DDOG shows the growth in its customer base over the past few years amid increasing market penetration and growing adoption of its products.

Heading into Q3 FY26 earnings, Bank of America analyst Koji Ikeda reiterated a buy rating on Datadog stock and raised his price target to $335 from $305. The analyst noted that industry checks and data indicate that infrastructure software demand is strengthening, with AI increasingly driving usage and growth.

Ikeda called Datadog and Atlassian his top picks, saying that companies that can show that AI is a tailwind to growth could see their valuation rerate, even at premium multiples. He added that the total addressable market for observability is growing faster than anticipated, driven by accelerated growth of enterprise AI and the rapid ramp of consumer AI agents.

The five-star analyst believes that focus will be on revenue growth, which he estimates to be 28.7% for Q3 FY26 versus the Street's consensus of 29.1%. Ikeda thinks that the market is underestimating the growth prospects of DDOG's security products. Meanwhile, he cautioned investors about concentration risk related to large customers.

"We think results will be supportive of our long-term thesis that Datadog is a strong share gainer in the rapidly expanding observability opportunity," said Ikeda.

Ikeda ranks No. 673 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 55% of the time, delivering an average return of 10.9%. See Datadog Statistics on TipRanks.

The next pick, semiconductor giant Nvidia, is also benefiting from AI-driven demand for its products. After attending Nvidia's GTC Berlin conference, Morgan Stanley analyst Joseph Moore reaffirmed a buy rating on NVDA stock with a price target of $300, calling it a top pick in the semiconductor space.

The five-star analyst said that the conference supported his thesis that Nvidia continues to effectively navigate bottlenecks in the supply chain, land/power/shell, and funding. In fact, Moore is "convinced that all 3 aspects are opportunities for NVDA."

While Nvidia's GTC Berlin offered no major updates, Moore noted the conference reflected that the company continues to add to its significant advantages heading into 2027. The analyst said that Vera Rubin is gaining momentum, though details on next year's Rubin Ultra were lighter than expected.

With demand outpacing supply, Moore said that focus is on closing the gap. In this regard, he highlighted the supply chain deals on substrate, packaging and testing. While the analyst continues to view TSMC 3 nm wafer supply as the biggest challenge, he expects it to ease over time.

Moore ranks No. 142 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 59% of the time, delivering an average return of 23.4%. See Nvidia Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Aerospace company SpaceX recently announced solid third-quarter results, with its AI revenue surging to $7.56 billion from $813 million in the prior-year quarter.

Impressed by the results, Needham analyst Ryan Koontz reiterated a buy rating on SPCX stock with a price target of $250. Koontz highlighted that SpaceX's Q3 results significantly exceeded expectations, proving that the company's vertically integrated business model is driving massive scale.

Furthermore, Koontz emphasized the 195.2% sequential growth in SpaceX's AI revenue, fueled by wholesale spot compute deals. He noted management's increasing focus on its AI strategy, given that it is optimistic about adding 10 gigawatts of incremental capacity during 2027.

#2910. The Celtics, in all of their early-season glory, haven’t passed Brad Stevens’ test yet

We're about a fifth of the way through the 2026-27 Boston Celtics season.

So far, mostly so good. The Celtics are 11-4, which has them slotted second in the East behind the Philadelphia 76ers — whom they beat in comfortable fashion last week at Xfinity Mobile Arena, 123-101.

For you analytics heads out there, Boston ranks sixth in offensive rating, second in defensive rating, and second in net rating.

Paul George, whom the Cs bet on when they traded Jaylen Brown for him (and some nice picks) couldn't be playing much better. If you need any insight into how the PG-13 Experience has been, look no further than the fanbase collectively referring to him as "Paul God."

The other offseason addition hasn't panned out as well, but we won't jump on the pile here.

Through this first month, the only real nitpick about this team is the schedule that they've had. Multiple games against the putrid Milwaukee Bucks and Chicago Bulls, plus a handful against other struggling squads like the Charlotte Hornets, Dallas Mavericks, Toronto Raptors, and Brooklyn Nets make the 11-4 record feel a bit inflated.

Don't take my word for it; here's what Brad Stevens had to say about last season's 56-win team in his end-of-season press conference.

"We have to play well in the regular season to get to where we want to go," he told reporters back in May. "We don't want to undersell the regular season. That said, as I look at us honestly, I look at how we played against the best teams is relevant and should be taken into account as we look at our team moving forward."

Boston's record against the top three teams in each conference was 3-11. Of course, their first-round loss to the 76ers only amplified that issue. The Sixers weren't a "top team" but at full-strength (which they miraculously were in Games 4-7), they're not your typical Play-In team.

Anyway, there was a clear correlation in all the losses vs. that in the wins. Boston shot just 27.4% from three in the losses. In victories, that number jumped up to 41.7%.

This season, they haven't quite shown us that it'll be a different story just yet. The Cs have had five games against teams above .500 and have won just two. Their three losses came agains the Detroit Pistons, Houston Rockets, and Denver Nuggets.

The two wins have featured elite shooting performances from the perimeter. Boston sank 16-40 in their home opener against the New York Knicks, and then lit the Sixers up with a 22-49 performance in Philly.

Somehow, they lost the opener against Detroit despite a strong shooting day, but the other two losses have gone exactly how you'd guess.

I know how this all sounds, too. Like yeah, of course they're going to shoot better in wins than losses — you could probably pull up the splits for every team in the league and they'd look the same.

With the Celtics, though, it's #DifferentHere. A team that's relied so heavily on winning the mathematical margins over the past four seasons has seen shooting variance end three of them in disappointing fashion. The 2024 team was special, but also managed to win playoff games when they went cold — even they dropped a few headscratchers thanks to variance.

Does this all mean that this season's team sucks?

Probably not. But it's disappointing that they've come up short a few times in their limited amount of "prove it" games. Their lost to Houston did come on the second night of a back-to-back where Paul God was resting. So, there's some grace to be given there.

One optimistic takeaway so far has been the elite defense. Boston's 106.0 defensive rating has been built on sharp rotations, disruptive defensive playmaking, and a strong paint presence from Neemias Queta. All three of those things will remain under their control regardless of whether or not the shots are going in on a given night.

The Celtics have held opponents to under 110 points in 11 of their 15 games thus far. By doing so, they're at least giving themselves a chance to get across the finish line when the threes don't fall. We might be watching them develop their backup plan in real time.

It's going to be a while before another "test" game. Their NBA Cup group stage finale against the Atlanta Hawks will be a minor test before they head to San Antonio to take on the Spurs next Sunday. A win against Wemby and the reigning West champs would go along way in battling the "schedule merchants" narratives.

#2908. 3 Falcons defenders hit with 4 fines from Week 10 loss to Chiefs

The Falcons racked up nine penalties in last week's lackluster loss to the Kansas City Chiefs, but they were all procedural — there weren't any flags for actions that typically lead to fines. But that didn't stop the NFL from issuing four fines total to three different defensive players stemming from last Sunday's action.

Defensive lineman Brandon Dorlus was the lucky guy who got hit with two fines, each for $7,114. One was for unsportsmanlike conduct/taunting, and one was for a late hit on a quarterback.

DeMarcco Hellams, who slid into the starting spot opened up by the Falcons' decision to trade Jessie Bates III to the Jaguars before the NFL's deadline, owes the NFL $6,509 for unnecessary roughness/a hit on a defenseless player, and linebacker Harold Perkins was also fined $5,203 for unnecessary roughness/use of the helmet.

The NFL splits the fine money it collects from players between the NFL Foundation to support the health, safety and wellness of athletes at all levels of football and the Professional Athletes Foundation to support NFL Legends in need.

#2904. From 1-5 to above .500, the Suns are doing something right

The Phoenix Suns are a game above .500. When this team started the season 1-5, I wasn't entirely convinced I'd have the opportunity to type those words anytime soon. Yet here we are, thanks to some improved chemistry, particularly among the starting unit, and a favorable stretch of the schedule.

How much of this resurgence can be attributed to genuine improvement versus the quality of competition? Only time will tell. We spend so much time dissecting how games are won or lost that we occasionally lose sight of a rather important detail. A win is a win. And in the NBA, every single one of them matters.

Yes, the Suns defeated the Utah Jazz by 10 points on Friday night, despite allowing Utah to trim the deficit to four in the closing minutes. Naturally, some will point to that late surge as evidence that this wasn't a particularly impressive victory. The funny thing? Many of those same people will dismiss Wednesday's win over the Sacramento Kings because, well, that's what the Suns were supposed to do. Don't be that guy.

Phoenix put itself in a position to win on Friday night, although Utah certainly helped the cause by missing 13 free throws. The result? A third consecutive road victory and a team that suddenly finds itself on the right side of .500.

Winning in the NBA is hard. I don't care who you're playing, how many injuries the opposing team is dealing with, or how ugly the basketball might look at times. Every team in this league has talented players capable of making your life miserable on any given night. Funny how that works, too. When an undermanned opponent beats the Suns, we're quick to praise their resilience and talk about how there's still some fight in that dog. But when Phoenix beats an undermanned opponent, suddenly the victory comes with an asterisk in the court of public opinion.

The win over Utah wasn't necessarily pretty. Some stretches made you uncomfortable, moments that invited frustration, and a fourth quarter that probably didn't need to be as interesting as it became. But it was effective. It added another victory to the win column, extended the momentum of a successful road trip, and gave the Suns something positive to carry into Monday's matchup against the New Orleans Pelicans.

It certainly beats the alternative. Never forget that alternative, either. Because if the Suns had blown that lead and lost to Utah, we'd be spending the next 48 hours dissecting every possession, questioning every rotation decision, and wondering whether this team had learned a damn thing from its early struggles.

Instead? Good win. Hop on the plane. On to the next city.

The Villain's 29 points on an efficient 12-of-18 gives him his second BSB of the season.

#2900. Europe's cyber weak spot is the everyday businesses nobody thought to protect

Cyberattacks on refrigerators in French supermarkets in October spurred Swedish chain Coop to act. But it wasn't fast enough.

Attackers hit refrigeration systems in seven of its stores in early November. The affected stores had been scheduled for security upgrades just two days later, a company spokesperson told CNBC.

Mundane but essential equipment, used by everyday businesses, has become a favorite target of cyber saboteurs in recent weeks.

Experts are warning that a combination of known vulnerabilities, basic security failures and ambiguity over whether suppliers or their clients are responsible are making things worse, as AI makes cyber threats faster and more sophisticated.

Guy Segal, CEO of cybersecurity firm Sygnia, told CNBC that his company handled 285% more incidents in October than a year earlier, with demand forcing it to turn away some businesses seeking emergency assistance.

"It's going to get worse before it's going to get better," Segal said.

The recent cyberattacks targeted refrigeration systems across hundreds of European supermarkets, remotely altering temperature settings and forcing retailers to discard spoiled food.

France has since accused a cyber unit with links to Iran's Revolutionary Guard Corps of the attacks, an allegation Tehran denies.

Fredrik Blix, a cybersecurity researcher at Stockholm University, told CNBC that thousands of industrial control systems remain accessible over the public internet, sometimes despite manufacturers' warnings.

Segal said that basic cyber protection measures such as multi-factor authentication and basic network segmentation are more important than ever.

He said risks to major civic infrastructure like the electrical grid were front of mind and so "usually pretty well protected."

"There are always those civil supply chain components that are way, way less protected. Could be supermarkets, could be gas stations, could be… the screens on the Tube," Segal said, referring to London's underground rail system.

The supermarket attacks showed "this is the new normal, and definitely it's a wake-up call for every company out there," Segal added.

Segal said that inexpensive AI tools mean technical expertise and resources are no longer needed to launch attacks. He described one recent incident in which clients suffered breaches within six hours of a vulnerability becoming known, as perpetrators used AI to act faster — a process that might have taken weeks a year earlier.

Segal, whose firm helps companies respond to breaches and strengthen their defenses, warned that businesses without prearranged incident-response support could struggle to secure emergency assistance, as overwhelmed cybersecurity firms prioritize existing clients.

In one case, he said, Sygnia had to turn down a company because no response teams were available. Segal added that the company in question had already been turned down by ten other providers.

Jacob Silverman, CEO of risk advisory firm Kroll, told CNBC earlier this month that the company was operating at full capacity in its incident-response business amid what he described as "the most rapid uptick in threat activity that we've ever seen."

Segal argued that retailers should treat cybersecurity risks with the same seriousness as fire safety and food hygiene, including when outsourcing maintenance.

When Ahold Delhaize stores in Belgium and Luxembourg were attacked on Nov. 2, it exposed how different independent store operators had responded to the warnings following the earlier attacks in France.

Retailers had been warned about the vulnerabilities following earlier attacks in France, and those that implemented the recommended protections were spared, CEO Frans Muller told investors in an earnings call earlier this month, but smaller operators with fewer technical resources had struggled to make the necessary changes.

Coop, whose stores are owned by regional consumer cooperatives or operated by franchises, told CNBC that public attention on the recent attacks was the main driver of security improvements. It said the vulnerability at the affected stores had since been addressed.

The financial consequences of cyberattacks can be substantial. Ahold Delhaize told investors in November that a separate October cyberattack on its online operations and supply chain would reduce fourth-quarter sales by 200 million euros ($219 million).

Danish shipping giant Maersk, which suffered an estimated $250 million to $300 million in losses from the 2017 NotPetya cyberattack, has spent nearly a decade strengthening its defenses.

CEO Vincent Clerc told CNBC earlier this month that the experience had prompted significant investment in security capabilities, but warned that AI was enabling attackers to become more effective.

"Everybody is in a race" to boost defenses, he said.

Research by cybersecurity firm Censys found that the number of internet-exposed refrigeration controllers made by Danfoss and Copeland exposed to cyber hacking fell 53% globally between Oct. 9 and Nov. 5.

However, more than 3,100 remained exposed, and Censys said it could not determine whether the decline reflected permanent security improvements or equipment simply being taken offline.

A Copeland spokesperson confirmed that some of its refrigeration systems were affected by the vulnerability and said it had contacted customers to reinforce cybersecurity guidance, including keeping devices off the public internet and updating software.

Danfoss didn't respond to CNBC requests for comment.

In the U.K., a covert cybersecurity operation has been underway to protect tens of thousands of refrigeration systems at supermarkets, warehouses, restaurants and other businesses, the BBC reported on Friday. After researchers discovered a major vulnerability in late October, cybersecurity specialists, IT teams and the U.K.'s National Cyber Security Centre worked to alert dozens of companies and secure their systems before hackers could exploit the flaw.

The EU's Cyber Resilience Act will also impose stricter security requirements on manufacturers of connected products from December 2027.

But the legislation does not require all existing equipment to be made compliant with the new standards.

Blix told CNBC on Nov. 6 that European authorities should take a more proactive approach to reducing vulnerabilities by identifying exposed industrial control systems and contacting their operators before attacks occur.

"What worries me is that we don't really do anything," he said, adding that "we are shuffling a lot of papers" and "doing a lot of risk assessments" but failing to take meaningful action.

#2897. A massive bearish trade just happened in the chip stocks

So much for buying the dip.As the S&P 500 entered correction territory and the Nasdaq-100 flirted with bear-market territory, down 19% from highs as of midday Friday, the single biggest options trade of the day was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH) has a long way more to fall.Around 12:30 E.T., someone spent $93 million buying 20,000 380-strike puts expiring Sep. 17, 2027, according to trading data reviewed by SpotGamma and Volland, options-analytics platforms that both show the trade as a likely opening position, with volume exceeding existing open interest in the contract. The trade bets that the index will fall below $380, a nearly 8% decline from its current level. The SMH is off 40% from its recent all-time highs.

"That trade stands out big-time," wrote Jason DeLorenzo, founder and owner of Volland. "It is either a hedge or speculative, but either way it isn't a vote of confidence."The puts were bought for $46.50 a piece, an arguably expensive choice for a downside bet with volatility in the ETF trading at 70.

"You're paying a metric ton of Vega for that thing," added SpotGamma's Brent Kochuba, referring to the contract's sensitivity to changes in volatility of the underlying chip ETF. "Maybe they were a forced buyer, or maybe they're actually selling this, which can still be the case even though the price shows it happening closer to the ask."

The semiconductor index headed for a 9% drop this week alone.

#2894. Top Wall Street analysts like these 3 dividend stocks for consistent income

Democrats' midterm election win has put the artificial intelligence trade in question, as voters clearly expressed opposition to the rapid buildout of data centers. The stock market could remain volatile amid AI trade concerns, macro uncertainties, and geopolitical tensions.

In this scenario, investors can add dividend-paying stocks to their portfolios to earn steady income. However, the extensive universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Top Wall Street analysts' recommendations can help investors shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as these experts assign ratings after in-depth financial analysis.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Viper Energy (VNOM) is a subsidiary of Diamondback and owns and acquires mineral and royalty interests, primarily in the Permian Basin. The company reported upbeat third-quarter earnings, supported by recent acquisitions and continued development activity from Diamondback and third-party operators. Viper announced a base cash dividend of 50 cents per share for Q3 2026, payable on Nov. 19. Based on the base and variable dividends announced over the past 12 months, VNOM's dividend yield stands at 5.7%.

Following the Q3 results, TD Cowen analyst Aaron Bilkoski reiterated a buy rating on Viper Energy stock with a price target of $61. The analyst noted that the company delivered solid Q3 results, with production, including oil and natural gas liquids, exceeding both TD Cowen and consensus estimates.

The 5-star analyst highlighted that the key update was the announcement of two royalty acquisitions for $809 million in cash, including deferred consideration, and $82 million of newly issued shares.

Given the recently announced base dividend hike, Bilkoski expects Viper to direct incremental free cash flow toward buybacks. While the acquisitions increase leverage, the analyst believes that Viper has enough capacity for opportunistic buybacks.

Bilkoski noted that Viper has historically delivered one of the highest production per share growth profiles in his royalty coverage, but still trades at a notable discount to the royalty peer average and the company's historical FCF yield range. "In our view, its above-average growth warrants a higher valuation," said the analyst.

Bilkoski ranks No. 525 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 57% of the time, delivering an average return of 12.80%. See Viper Energy Stock Buybacks on TipRanks.

Exxon Mobil (XOM) reported robust Q3 2026 profits, as its U.S. refineries boosted production to capitalize on the high diesel fuel prices sparked by the conflict in the Middle East. The oil and gas giant announced a quarterly dividend of $1.07 per share, payable on Dec. 10, reflecting a 3.9% increase. This marks Exxon's 44th consecutive year of dividend increases. At an annualized dividend of $4.28 per share, XOM offers a yield of 2.53%.

Impressed by the Q3 print, TD Cowen analyst Jason Gabelman reiterated a buy rating on Exxon stock and raised his price target to $193 from $180 to reflect higher downstream earnings, with the expectation of increased mid-cycle margins.

Gabelman noted that the strength in Exxon's refining business continues to offset Middle East-related headwinds. He added that the company increased its ownership in LNG projects, which should reduce its Middle East concentration over time from 75% to 50% and lower the likelihood of diversification-focused mergers and acquisitions. Beyond LNG, Gabelman expects Exxon's growth in the 2030s to come from Guyana, Kashagan, Permian, and possibly Venezuela.

Gabelman noted that Exxon announced a dividend hike of nearly 4% and plans to maintain buybacks at $20 billion for 2027. "The decision to maintain reduces the pro-cyclicality nature of the buyback," said the analyst.

Gabelman ranks No. 347 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 66% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.90%. See Exxon Mobil Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

Williams (WMB), which owns and operates energy infrastructure to deliver natural gas, reported strong third-quarter results. The company announced a quarterly dividend of $0.525 per share, or $2.10 per share annualized, payable on Dec. 28. WMB offers a dividend yield of 3.09%.

In reaction to the Q3 2026 results, CIBC analyst Robert Catellier reaffirmed a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $82. The analyst noted that Q3 results were essentially in line with expectations and reflected continued strength in execution across WMB's infrastructure portfolio.

Catellier said that Williams' Q3 performance was driven by higher transmission, storage, and power generation revenues, as well as contributions from the Momentum Midstream acquisition.

He noted that Williams maintained its full-year EBITDA guidance of $8.3 billion to $8.5 billion. In fact, results are tracking toward the higher end of the guidance range, backed by contributions from recently completed projects and the Momentum Midstream deal. WMB continues to target long-term EBITDA compound annual growth rate of at least 11% through 2030.

Catellier expects WMB's long-term targets to be supported by incremental transmission and storage opportunities, including Transco's Southeast Supply Enhancement II project and an 84 MMcf/d expansion of the Garden Connector project.

#2892. NBA: Top 5 Wolves Games I’ve Attended

Over the years, I've been very blessed to attend a litany of exciting Timberwolves games, many in the playoffs. Now being a Gen-Z'er, these all came in the Anthony Edwards era. I wish I could've seen Kevin Garnett live, but unfortunately his prime came before my time as a serious basketball watcher.

Here are my top five games that I've attended and my point-of-view, in reverse order.

The entire 2023-24 season was an absolute whirlwind and the lead up to this game was no different. I was entirely confident the Wolves were going to blitz the Dallas Mavericks as we'd advance to the NBA Finals, but alas that was not the case. I cannot begin the immense joy I felt walking into Target Center that night, with pregame drinks at Cowboy Jack's across the street, of course. I hit the team store to get some Conference Finals merch, took some pictures of the TNT crew (including Draymond Green, ew), and even got to my seat damn near in the rafters.

It was pure bliss, only to get my heart ripped out two and a half hours later. Even though Minnesota lost, it was still a close game with high stakes and as just a fan of the sport, I was enthralled. Had the Wolves won this game it would've been higher, but it lands on my list at number five.

The story of my time at this game begins at Blue Door Pub on Como Avenue in Minneapolis. Only living a couple blocks away, I had never been and my time there was spent nervously eating a Juicy Lucy and worrying about the game. If you may remember, Phoenix was widely thought of as the prohibitive favorite. Guys like Devin Booker and Kevin Durant terrified me, as to that point the Timberwolves hadn't won a playoff series since 2004.

The Wolves absolutely took over. Anthony Edwards was hitting step-back threes over Durant while jawing at him. Naz Reid was hitting "jelly" reverse layups on the fastbreak. I almost couldn't believe it. Watching your team win like that, especially live…there's nothing like it. Because I attended this game and it went well, a tradition was sparked with my roommates and I: Since we had watched the game apart, I now had to watch the games alone and away from them to preserve those good vibes. I can't say it didn't work, either, as I watched games two, three, and four from my room as Minnesota swept Phoenix.

Man, this was fun. For the first time in my basketball watching life, a Timberwolves team enjoyed success in a playoff setting. Riding some irrational confidence, I didn't think that there was any way that a LA Clippers team led by Paul George (still called "Pandemic P" at the time) was going to stop the Wolves from setting a date with the Memphis Grizzlies in the first round.

George had a great game, hitting big shot after big shot. Things weren't exactly going great on the Timberwolves side of things because if you remember, Karl-Anthony Towns was in major foul trouble for most of the game. Then D'Angelo Russell started making shots. Anthony Edwards made a couple big plays. Before I knew it, I was watching Patrick Beverly throw the ball up in the air as the buzzer sounds and jump up onto the scorer's table like the Wolves had just won the NBA Finals.

To all the NBA fans who clown us Wolves fans for that reaction and our appreciation of it: take a hike. You never had to experience the suck that was a Luke Ridnour-Alexey Shved backcourt. As far as meaningful games I've attended, this may take the top spot. However, this list is the best games, so it lands at number three. I say meaningful because I attended this game with just my dad. He is the one who raised me to be a basketball fan and taught me most of what I know about the game, and to share the moment of our favorite team advancing for the first time since I was two years old with him was absolutely special. I'll never forget it.

The most recent game on my list slides in at number two. Anthony Edwards was out. Donte DiVincenzo had torn his Achille's. Then just hours before the game, it was announced that Ayo Dosunmu was going to be out. I was convinced that Jaden McDaniels and Julius Randle were not going to be enough to finish the Nuggets with most of their cast of characters. Denver would be able to focus their efforts on Jaden, who earlier in the series had dropped the "They're all bad defenders" line.

In the building, it felt like most Timberwolves fans felt the same. Minnesota blitzed Denver early as Julius made some tough buckets flanked by TJ Shannon being a menace in transition. Then as the game went on and the Wolves remained neck and neck with the Nuggets, even earning a lead, belief started to creep in. Denver wasn't going away though. I really thought they were going to pull some voodoo magic out of Jokić's horse racing hat and push this series to a seventh game. I was pushed further down this line of thinking when Jokić and Jaylen Clark got into a scuffle, resulting in two Minnesota technical fouls while Denver only received one.

Then, Jaden McDaniels showed everyone the player he truly is.

A couple made threes, a couple mir-range shots, and plenty of drives to the rim where he proved himself right: the Nuggets were all bad defenders. 32 points and 10 rebounds for Jaden that night, and a massive statement to the rest of the league that even though the Wolves were down their best three guards, their pack was not left defenseless.

Yes, the best game I have ever attended in person is a 45-point blowout victory.

Everyone remembers this series, but they remember it for the epic Game 7 comeback. What gets lost in the shuffle is that the Timberwolves blitzed Denver to the tune of a 2-0 series lead before the Nuggets came back with a Balboa-level haymaker to take a series lead of 3-2. This Game 6 was an elimination game for Minnesota, and it didn't start very well. The Nuggets jumped out to a 9-2 lead after just three minutes of play.

What followed that opening stanza of that game can only be described as a waxing.

Blink and you would've missed a 24-2 Timberwolves run. The Nuggets made a couple runs, sure, but it was never all that close again. Hell, even Jordan McLaughlin and Luka Garza got minutes on the floor in a Game 6 in the playoffs. That's how lopsided this victory was. Euphoria is the only feeling I can describe from the crowd at Target Center that night. Every Wolves fan wearing the "I <3 Wolves" t-shirt left happy that night. The team we absolutely hate had a chance to knock us off our Cinderella ride early, on our floor even. Saying "Hell No" and forcing a Game 7 was the only thing we could do, and we did it in crushing fashion.

What's the best Wolves game you've ever attended? Do y'all want to see more top five lists in the future? Sound off in the comments below.

#2890. Why Anthropic's blockbuster IPO couldn't escape the stock market correction

No one can say the Anthropic initial public offering wasn't a success for the company. Deal size and pricing were signs of what IPO experts say was insatiable demand for "ANT" shares. But what came within a day is something that investors better get used to: anxiety in the stock market about valuations being assigned to artificial intelligence revenue and business models that should keep Anthropic shares volatile in the period ahead.

The Anthropic IPO reinforces those fears rather than running counter to them, says Avery Marquez, director of investment strategies at Renaissance Capital, which researches IPOs and manages the Renaissance IPO ETF (IPO).

"The volatility in the AI trade we've been seeing for the past few months stems from valuations and whether we are in a bubble, and that's been heightened and highlighted by the Anthropic IPO," Marquez said on "ETF Edge" on Friday.

And in the case of Anthropic, which finished trading Friday down 9%, the valuation anxiety becomes "so much more prevalent," she added, because it is a new stock.

It is not just any new stock, but one that has Wall Street adding downside risks to research notes likely never seen before, and which may be the greatest understatement (or most subtle equities research joke) in the history of earnings analysis. "Anthropic could develop AI systems which become fully autonomous and wipe out humanity which may lead to a serious decline in earnings," Seaport Research Partners analyst wrote Jay Goldberg wrote on Nov. 18.

"It was challenging company to value ... and it's very richly valued," Marquez said. "We're seeing something more reasonable and investors grappling now with where should this company actually be valued," she added.

The fact that Anthropic pulled off the deal in this market and was able to command the price it did was an achievement, according to Yuri Khodjamirian, chief investment officer at Tema ETFs, which offers multiple funds that invest in pre-IPO shares of companies and held Anthropic in its Tema Photonics & Optical ETF (LAZR) well before IPO day. "We have to remember they pulled off a very large IPO in the middle of a very severe market with the volatility in the tech sector specifically," Khodjamirian said on "ETF Edge."

Khodjamirian says the investor demand for AI remains, but amid a reassessment of returns. And he says an equally hard task has now begun for Anthropic. Investors are unwinding positions in everything from tech stocks to gold and bitcoin, and repositioning for 2027 based on their new outlooks. "We are in a bit of vacuum," he said.

"From here on the market is trying trying to figure out what direction to go in," Khodjamirian said, whether that is based on Fed policy, geopolitics, the midterm elections results, or the AI trade. "In many cases, the chief investment officers I speak to, and the clients, say 'I'm just gonna take some risk off and wait and see until I get more info.' And with the Anthropic, the revenue picture and how the AI adoption curve progresses will be a vital stat to watch," he added.

Plenty of value has already been wiped out in tech, according to Khodjamirian, but investors need new signals to buy. That doesn't just go for Anthropic but for much of the market's tech leadership. "One of the things most difficult for investors to understand right now is some of the leadership in the market that has been taken by semiconductor companies," he said, adding that the peak was 22% of S&P 500 earnings. But these companies, like Nvidia and Micron, have a very different earnings profile from the rest of the market.

The VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH), which was down over 10% on Friday and around 15% for the week, was on pace for its worst week since September 2001, according to CNBC data on Friday afternoon. It also lost 15% in one week of March 2020. The tech sector of the S&P 500, tracked by the State Street Technology Select Sector SPDR ETF (XLK), turned in its worst weekly performance since the Covid crash.

Khodjamirian said while these stocks may look cheap on a P/E basis right now after the heavy selling, that requires faith in the earnings estimates, which he says will be more important as results are released for the next few years. "Right now it looks cheap, but will companies like Micron and Nvidia actually deliver?"

Nvidia shares fell 14% on Friday, adding to its worst stretch of weekly performance since 2008. What's more, the single biggest trade in the options market on Friday was a near $100-million bet that the VanEck Semiconductor ETF has a long way more to fall.

And the same caution about the outlook goes for Anthropic, he said. "That's why we need to watch Anthropic's annual recurring revenue number, because it is a key part of puzzle," he said.

Marquez said there is always hype in names like SpaceX and Anthropic when they go public, and it is hard to know whether the pricing of the deal is based on fundamentals, "but we just need more time and lots price discovery," she said. "It is not going to happen overnight," she added.

As an IPO researcher, she says this "discernment" is a healthy thing for the markets. There are few cases to look to as comps for Anthropic, other than SpaceX, and before that, way back to Facebook's IPO in 2012. And neither example is one of quick recovery. SpaceX shares remain well below their first-day closing price more than five months after its IPO. Facebook (now Meta), meanwhile, "did not do well for the first year after going public and we see where it is now. It's just the nature of these disruptive tech companies that come to market," Marquez said. "They lend themselves to more volatility and a longer period of price discovery for investors."

All eyes will next be on Anthropic's rival OpenAI, which has suggested it will be ready to go public in 2027. Marquez said the reaction to the Anthropic deal may be a signal that it was "a good idea to wait, allowing themselves to build more quarters of better financials and improve fundamentals a bit before taking on a market that is really quite discerning."

Tema does not own shares of OpenAI in any of its ETFs. But, "I can't say it wont be," Khodjamirian said. "They exist out there in the secondary market, and we will look at it, but we haven't made any decision. It depends on the ETF fit and if the portfolio manager wants to invest. We are always looking out for these interesting businesses but never just for the sake of private exposure," he added.

Tema does own Databricks, Stripe, Kalshi and Polymarket across its funds. It also was an investor in SpaceX pre-IPO through its Tema Space Innovators ETF (NASA).

Khodjamirian said demand from public market investors to access these "generational businesses" will continue to be the case, despite the market volatility, and will more often come before IPO day. In fact, he pointed to the filing from the world's largest asset manager and one of the biggest ETF companies in the world, BlackRock, earlier this week. It wants to bring out a new kind of exchange-traded product that would bring the ETF and interval fund structures together as a way to increase exposure to private companies, with shares trading on an exchange while the fund makes monthly repurchase offers.

#2887. An early breakdown in the polar vortex is threatening already depleted gas stocks

An unprecedented early breakdown of the stratospheric polar vortex and a record-breaking El Niño event has put energy traders on alert this winter, with analysts warning the weather and climate phenomena could have an outsized impact on Europe.

Meteorologists are currently observing a record early breakdown of the polar vortex, a large-scale circulation of strong winds that typically keeps extremely cold air over the North Pole.

Changes in the strength and shape of the polar vortex can influence weather patterns much lower down in the atmosphere, potentially bringing in colder weather to parts of Europe and North America.

This year, however, the collapse of the stratospheric polar vortex, or SPV, is taking place at the same time as an unusually powerful El Niño, a climate event that can boost global temperatures and disrupt rainfall.

The collision of these two events raises the risk of a prolonged cold spell that could lift heating demand and force European countries to draw down already-low reserves faster. That would leave the continent more reliant on imported liquefied natural gas (LNG) at a time when supplies from the Middle East are disrupted.

To be sure, Europe's natural gas storage levels started the winter at their lowest level in more than a decade.

The International Energy Agency warned earlier this month that low European storage levels and limited storage options in several LNG-importing countries in Asia and South America could intensify the competition for cargoes and put upward pressure on prices during periods of high demand.

Simon Lee, a lecturer in atmospheric science at the U.K.'s University of St Andrews, said the combination of a sudden stratospheric warming (SSW) with an El Niño event is not necessarily unusual, but meteorologists had been expecting a polar vortex breakdown in February — not as early as November.

The early SPV disruption is an "extremely rare" event that could influence winter weather in northwest Europe and the eastern U.S., Lee said, increasing the chance of colder conditions although likely not to the extent that a polar vortex event caused parts of Niagara Falls to partially freeze in January 2014.

"Geopolitics and weather are the two key volatility drives for energy and now they seem to be happening at the same time — and that's always the biggest risk for markets," Florence Schmit, senior energy strategist at Rabobank, told CNBC.

For months, meteorologists expected an El Niño event would result in a milder winter in Europe and relatively muted heating demand, Schmit said, which was broadly seen as welcome news given ongoing concerns over the continent's tight regional supplies for gas and crude oil.

The prospect of a colder-than-expected winter due to an early breakdown of the polar vortex could be "incredibly difficult" for Europe to navigate, Schmit warned. She noted that the region's gas storages have seen "very, very strong withdrawals" over the last two weeks, "which is quite unusual for this time of year."

"If you continue to see the same storage withdrawals over the next couple of months, that means energy prices will spike again — and they won't spike because of geopolitics. This time it will be the weather driving that spike and that volatility," Schmit said.

European gas prices have soared to levels not seen since the 2022 energy crisis in recent weeks, notching 103.9 euros ($113.99) per megawatt hour in late October. European benchmark Dutch TTF futures for December delivery stood at around 87.28 euros on Friday afternoon.

"We have never seen an early breakdown of the polar vortex and a super el Nino in the same winter so we have no historical precedent on how this will impact global gas demand," David Lewis, principal research analyst of gas and liquified natural gas at Wood Mackenzie, told CNBC by email.

El Niño is commonly associated with milder winters, while a SSW event typically leads to colder temperatures and higher snowfall, Lewis said, noting this could materialize in December and January within Europe.

"So far, the start of the European winter heating season has been quite cold but more importantly has seen low wind speeds across Northern Europe," Lewis said.

"The drawdown in stocks in the first half of November has been amongst the highest on record. With inventories starting the winter at their lowest level on record, any further deterioration in temperatures or wind speeds will have an outsized impact on European balances and lead to higher prices across Europe," he added.

#2881. The Best is Yet to Come for Caleb Williams and the Bears’ Offense

The Chicago Bears' bye week landed as close to the halfway point of the NFL season as possible, with 17 games on the schedule, giving us an opportunity to check back in with the team's offensive playmakers ahead of this week's matchup with New Orleans. A chaotic first few weeks saw slow starts from ascending pass-catchers Rome Odunze, Luther Burden, and Colston Loveland, absences from Caleb Williams and Kyle Monangai, and worst of all, a season-ending injury to Cole Kmet. Despite the waves created by the acquisition of defensive star Jeffery Simmons, this team was built with Ben Johnson's offensive identity at the forefront. This latest edition of Benchmarking Da Bears will serve as a reminder of just that:

Comp Player: 2022 Jared Goff (Detroit Lions)

Caleb Williams came out of the gate on fire in Carolina, leading the Bears to a 59-point offensive explosion in their 2026 debut. Unfortunately, the following week he faced a torrential downpour, Brian Flores' Vikings defense, and a hamstring injury that would keep him out for three games. Since his return, the Bears have not trailed. That launched Williams into the MVP conversation, and even had him near the front of the crowd until an ugly 4 interception game against Tampa Bay took a little wind out of his sails. Still, Williams has been playing extremely well, and there's reason to believe he can keep up this high-level of play in the latter half of the season.

Comp Player: 2025 D'Andre Swift (Chicago Bears)

One half of the Bears' admired tandem, Swift has been the lone back standing in recent weeks as Kyle Monangai continues to recover from his bout with turf toe. Swift hasn't looked completely healthy, which has reflected in his yards per carry numbers, but he's managed to power through and produced some much-needed scores for Chicago anyway. Swift has already set a new career high in rushing touchdowns (10), with a hat trick in Seattle serving as a monument to his and the offense as a whole's ability to commit to the run game.

Comp Player: 2023 Sam LaPorta (Detroit Lions)

Speaking of injuries, I'd be remiss not to mention the loss of Cole Kmet. Kmet went down for the year in the Bears' Thursday night victory over the New England Patriots, leaving them with gaps to fill in both the blocking and receiving game. It would be a stretch to say they've found a solution yet, but Colston Loveland could help if he's able to find some of the end-of-season form he showed last year. He's started slow in back-to-back seasons; can we expect him to elevate once again?

Comp Player: 2024 Jameson Williams (Detroit Lions)

Comp Player: 2022 Amon-Ra St. Brown (Detroit Lions)

Comp Player: 2022 Kalif Raymond (Detroit Lions)

The Bears' top trio of receivers have taken turns stepping up to the plate this season. Despite a disappointing start, Rome Odunze has shown a lot of flashes in recent weeks, particularly when it matters most. He's helped seal the game against New England and secure an early lead against Tampa Bay – which turned out to be pretty key. While his catch rate and lack of scores are less than ideal, his yardage is there, and as he grows more comfortable, we can hope to see continued improvements from Rome. In similar ways, Raymond started the year off as the Bears' go-to pass-catcher and Burden has had sporadic bursts throughout. If any or all of these three can find some consistency in the latter half of the year, the offense can certainly elevate to a new level at the right time.

The Saints are the first team up after the bye week for Chicago, but after that, they start what has shaped up to be the most intimidating stretch of any teams' schedule this season. In the NFC North, they may need to do exactly what Ben Johnson's preached and play their best football starting in November and December in order to even secure a spot in the playoffs.

#2879. Zscaler stock jumps 18% on report that CrowdStrike in talks to buy security vendor

Zscaler shares surged 18% in extended trading on Friday after Bloomberg reported that CrowdStrike is in talks to buy the cybersecurity vendor. Shares of CrowdStrike shares fell more than 8%.

The talks between the two companies are ongoing, Bloomberg reported, citing sources familiar in the matter. CrowdStrike declined to comment on the report. Zscaler didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

CrowdStrike has been one of the big cybersecurity winners in the artificial intelligence boom, with its stock about doubling in value this year, lifting its market cap to $235 billion. Zscaler, meanwhile, is down 13% this year, pushing its market cap down to about $32 billion.

Along with CrowdStrike, Palo Alto Networks has seen its business boom as businesses race to upgrade their networks in the AI era. Palo Alto closed Friday at a nearly $282 billion market cap.

Should a deal take place, it would be among the largest in the history of the cybersecurity industry. Google acquired Wiz last year for $32 billion. In 2024, Cisco paid about $28 billion for Splunk. Earlier this year, Palo Alto closed its purchase of CyberArk for $25 billion.

CrowdStrike's deals to this point have been on the smaller side.The company announced plans earlier this month to acquire agentic observability platform Fiddler AI and AI trust platform Dreadnode.

#2878. I never wanted the American Dream and I felt like something was wrong with me—until I moved abroad

I got my first job at 17, working the register at an Eckerd's drugstore in Texas. My manager was only a few years older than me, and while my coworkers seemed motivated by the idea of working their way up to his position, I felt no desire to follow that path.

It all felt suffocating: the added responsibility, the longer hours, the idea that this was the ladder I was supposed to want to climb. I wanted to work, but I couldn't wrap my head around building my whole identity around a job.

I never wanted the American Dream I saw so many people around me striving to achieve. For a long time, I wondered if something was wrong with me. It wasn't until I finally made a long-term move abroad about a decade ago that I realized it was okay to want something different.

After college, I got a job in travel sales. I was good at it. Talking about travel all day suited the wanderlust I'd carried since my first trip abroad in my teens.

I continued working in sales, marketing and the odd temp role for years. But they were always just jobs. I didn't feel like they defined me or feel a calling to an industry or title the way people around me seemed to.

Of course, I wanted financial stability, but I was unwilling to sacrifice my time and well-being to get it. I just didn't connect with the version of ambition that meant grinding toward a corner office. What I wanted the most wasn't status; it was freedom, growth and new experiences.

The wanderlust eventually caught up to me, and at 26 I moved to Spain to teach English. Even though I was working, people saw it as taking a year off from my career and warned me it could damage my resume. I simply didn't care. Living for my resume had never made sense to me.

Looking back, I think being abroad offered an escape from the pressure to chase the traditional path of the American Dream. There, I felt free to enjoy life and figure out who I was without worrying about what box I needed to tick next.

I wanted to stay in Spain, but I couldn't find a visa that would let me live there long-term, so I returned to the U.S. I became a serial job hopper for almost 10 years, quitting a job every year to spend summers visiting friends in the Netherlands, the U.K. and Switzerland or solo traveling in Spain on the money I'd saved up.

On the personal side, I wasn't in a rush to get married or have kids. In my 30s, single and childfree, I felt like I was failing at adulthood, even though deep down, I didn't actually want the things I was supposedly behind on. Settling down just because everyone else was doing it never felt right to me.

And I couldn't shake the dream of living in Europe. In my 20s, people mostly assumed my desire to travel was a phase. By my 30s, the conversations had shifted and I was getting pulled aside. Friends, family and colleagues all had some version of the same message: It was time to get serious and build a life in Texas.

But my gut said to try one more time. So I did.

In 2015, on my fourth attempt, I finally made it to Spain for good. It was scary to shake things up when everyone around me was settling down, but deep down I knew at 35, this was my chance to stop trying to conform and start building a life that felt right for me.

In my new home, the pace was slower. I quickly noticed that people actually took their vacations. Strangers at a dinner party didn't immediately ask me what I did for work. Adults in their 30s, 40s and beyond were out living full social lives. Single, childfree women my age didn't feel like an anomaly.

Living abroad didn't solve everything overnight. I still had to figure out how to support myself. But having the space to define myself professionally without constant judgment changed what felt possible.

In 2017, I launched She Hit Refresh, where I help women over 30 figure out how to move to Europe and build new lives for themselves. It's not something I could have created from anywhere else, with any other experience than my own.

In the process, I've met so many others who left corporate America behind to create something different for themselves. I've seen more women asking the same questions I spent years asking myself. Burnt out, disillusioned with hustle culture, realizing that checking all the boxes of the American Dream they grew up on didn't deliver the feeling they were promised.

We're all figuring it out together; each of us writing a version of a life that didn't fit a standard template. Moving abroad helped me understand that success, for me, was never about earning more or owning more. It was always about more ease, more time, more space, more life.

For years, I thought I was behind. I felt like something was wrong with me for wanting a different kind of life. Looking back, I just hadn't found my place yet.

Cepee Tabibian is the founder of She Hit Refresh, where she's helped over 24,000 women age 30+ plan their move abroad. Grab her free Move Abroad After 30 Guide to start planning your own move. The daughter of Colombian and Iranian immigrants, Cepee grew up in Houston, Texas, before becoming an immigrant herself in Spain. Follow her @shehitrefresh.

#2877. One ‘insane’ five-minute stretch shows just how lost Mitchell Robinson is with Celtics

Mitchell Robinson looks like his heart is still in New York.

The Celtics' free-agent acquisition has looked lost in Boston this season, struggling on both ends of the floor and turning the ball over at a career-high rate.

Robinson's troubles reached a new level during a bizarre stretch Wednesday in the third quarter of an eventual 105-101 Nuggets victory.

"Robinson wasn't ready for it on the short roll," play-by-play man Mike Monaco said on the broadcast.

Boston Celtics center Mitchell Robinson, front, goes to the basket as Denver Nuggets center Nikola Jokic chases him on Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026, in Denver. AP Photo/David Zalubowski

"And I don't know if that was a great pass to Mitchell Robinson on the roll," Richard Jefferson said on the call. "I don't think that's the best place to utilize him."

The Celtics had nine turnovers in the third quarter, and while only one was credited to Robinson officially, he was involved in at least three of them.

One was an offensive foul due to a moving screen (officially given to Payton Pritchard) and two of the others were due to poor communication between him and the ball handlers.

On one sequence with six minutes remaining in the third quarter, Robinson mishandled a pick-and-roll pass, leading to a dunk at the other end for Denver.

Paul George later attempted a pass to him wide open under the basket but Robinson stopped his movement early, and the ball sailed into the stands, drawing a few comments and confused looks from George.

Then with roughly 2:40 remaining in the quarter, Robinson was switching off a cutting Christian Braun for an easy basket.

Robinson was immediately given the hook after that play, after playing roughly five minutes in a disaster quarter, with Robinson's picks heavily involved in plays that ended in turnovers.

Perhaps coach Joe Mazzulla is having a tough time figuring out how to use Robinson, as he may not be the best fit as a rim runner and pick-and-roll specialist for dunks and passes to slashers.

Boston Celtics center Mitchell Robinson has been a turnover machine this season posting career-worst numbers. IMAGN IMAGES via Reuters Connect

"I think he's playing a role that we need him to play. It's bigs by committee," Mazzulla said after Monday's 115-105 loss to the Magic.

Those watching — including Sam Vecenie, host of the basketball podcast "Game Theory" — felt a little more strongly.

"I am truly blown away by the five minutes that Mitchell Robinson played in the 3rd quarter for Boston against Denver last night," Vecenie said of Robinson. "That was insane."

Payton Pritchard and head coach Joe Mazzulla of the Boston Celtics look on during a game against the Denver Nuggets on Wednesday, Nov. 18, 2026, in Denver. NBAE via Getty Images

Robinson's struggles go well beyond that, though. He has posted a terrible -30 plus-minus during his 246 minutes on the floor for the Celtics this year.

With him off the floor, the Celtics are +191, a massive 221-point swing.

Robinson has career-lows in rebounds per game (5.4) and an alarming 2.4 offensive rebounds per game, compared to 4.2 last year in just two fewer minutes per game for the Knicks' title team.

He's also posted a career-high 0.9 turnovers per game, and he has by far his lowest block and steal rates.

It will be interesting to see how Mazzulla gets Robinson acclimated, as he signed a three-year, $47.38 million contract this offseason. He was the one significant piece to leave the Knicks' title team as they were hellbent on not flirting with the second apron.

Celtics super fan Bill Simmons posted his concerns on X on Friday.

"'Mitchell Robinson will be fine, settle down, he just needs to play himself into shape' — a text I've sent 52 times this season already," Simmons said in a social media post.

The Celtics have been better without Mitchell Robinson on the floor. AP Photo/David Zalubowski

The Knicks have, in turn, replaced him with Andre Drummond, who is mostly there for rebounding, while former Warriors draft bust James Wiseman has also been getting some minutes at center.

The Celtics are 11-4, while the Knicks are 9-6.

#2875. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."
