#2853. Hawks excel off of turnovers, eliminate Hornets in NBA Cup win

The Atlanta Hawks picked up another victory in the NBA Cup on Friday night, eliminating the Charlotte Hornets from cup progression with a 111-97 victory at State Farm Arena. Jalen Johnson led the Hawks with 26 points while Aaron Wiggins added 19 points. For the Hornets, Moussa Diabate scored 21 points, Brandon Miller added 19 points.

After suffering a disappointing loss to the Bulls in overtime in their last NBA Cup appearance, the Hawks were presented a good opportunity to steady their position in the standings with a home tie against a struggling Charlotte side. Naturally, it was the visitors who were in the ascendancy for the majority of the first half. Rallying behind their size, the Hornets secured 12 offensive rebounds leading to 20 second chance points in the first half alone, contributing to the Hornets taking not just double-digit lead in the second quarter but a lead that ran as high as 14 points.

"Diabate was part of it," said Hawks head coach Quin Snyder of the Hornets' rebounding prowess. "Not only do they have he and Kalkbrenner, two guys that it's very difficult to keep them off glass by yourself. If you are able to put a second body on, which we didn't do enough. When we did, he may not get it, but then everybody's got to clean it up. The first part of that, we need to do better. The second part of it, I'd like to get to the second part where he actually doesn't get it (the rebound). A credit to him as far as how relentless he is on the glass. I thought as the game went on, we had some possessions, and those would be guard rebounds. I think RayJ got five (rebounds), he's the smallest guy on the court. We've got to have everybody in the mix to be effective on the defensive glass."

A cold stretch from the Hornets allowed the Hawks the opportunity to chip away at the lead; by the end of which the Hawks had whittled the lead down to just two points heading into the locker room. This momentum carried through into the second half, with the Hawks not only to re-take the lead but established a double-digit lead by the end of the third quarter, progressing to a lead as high as 19 points in the fourth quarter. While a Hornets run briefly brought the lead down to 10 points, it never dipped below double-digits and the Hawks would go on to secure a comfortable victory.

Postgame, Snyder was asked about the Hornets being limited to 40 second half points, and outlined the details that the Hawks tightened up on in the second half.

"I think our rotations were not sharp early, we were just a step behind," said Snyder of the second half. "That's really important for us in our pick-and-roll defense. As we're trying to keep the ball out of the paint, we're going to find ourselves in those situations. Some of it was close-outs, when we switched pick-and-roll we weren't aggressive enough early. They shot just right behind the screen. I just think we weren't as sharp as we needed to be."

One of the keys to victory in this contest were the Hawks' points off of turnovers, scoring 36 off of the Hornets' 17 turnovers.

On the drive and pass in the air from Coby White, Dyson Daniels pounces on the loose pass, and shovels the ball ahead to Nickeil Alexander-Walker for the basket:

On this drive, it's Jalen Johnson who comes up with the knockaway, and leads the break himself in transition to dunk at the rim:

Here, Moussa Diabate operates looking for a hand-off, and his pass is telegraphed by Aaron Wiggins, who escapes Naz Reid to finish at the rim:

Again, the Hawks are active on the perimeter and Daniels procures another steal and hands it off to Alexander-Walker, who pitches it to Johnson, who finishes at the rim again:

RayJ Dennis and Zuby Ejiofor combine on this defensive play to produce another Hornets turnover, from which Dennis gets into his runner for another basket in the third quarter:

Another knock-away from Dennis sets up another Atlanta fastbreak, this time Dennis lifts the alley-oop to Ejiofor for a highlight play:

With Kingston Flemings, Jock Landale, Asa Newell, and Mo Gueye absent, there were opportunities that may not normally be available for RayJ Dennis and Zuby Ejiofor. Both players made meaningful impacts in their time on the court, and we've looked at a few already with the turnovers.

Ejiofor played 13 minutes and scored five points, but his impact goes beyond the boxscore as Snyder outlined postgame.

"I think it's timely as well," said Snyder of Ejiofor's energy off the bench. "There's plays, whether it's a block— you see him dunk but what you don't notice all the time is he sprinted to get to that position. Those are the things ,in my mind, that are hard, because he's running without the promise of a reward. Sometimes for gratification, it's hard. The things that our team needs to do to be good, that's consistent, there's not a payout in the moment. You have to run, even when you don't get the ball, you have to get a hit, even when you don't get the rebound, you have to space, even if you don't get the shot. Those things are important to him. As a result, he puts himself in position to make those kinds of plays. It's the combination of the two things together because what he's doing is selfless, and then he actually gets rewarded for it. It's a really good thing. It's something we talk about and I want our whole team to emulate.When we do it, we're a much better team."

The play Snyder was referring to, I believe, is this one, where Ejiofor runs the floor and receives the pass for the dunk in transition:

Ejiofor did not back down when confronted with Diabate while on the floor; it was this, in addition to the energy he provided, that stood out to the Hawks last night.

"It was good, rook coming in, you know, not backing down," said Dyson Daniels of Ejiofor. "It's what we like, another big fella getting the mix with Mo (Gueye) being out, that's usually Mo doing that sort of thing. So, Zuby in there, it was really cool to see. He's just come in, giving us really good minutes, giving us good energy around the floor, and he's making some big shots for us. He's really stepped up since Jock's gone down, which has been good."

Dennis, similarly, played 13 minutes, scoring eight points but, similar to Ejiofor, his impact was felt beyond his boxscore.

"It's great when you have guys in the program," said Snyder of RayJ Dennis. "Last year in College Park, this summer, throughout the course of September, he's got a good feel for how we want to play. He played well in Oklahoma City, is all he didn't make a shot. He can get in the lane and when he gets in there he's capable of finishing. He also has good vision, I think he's worked on that, too. Big minutes from him tonight gave us a lift."

Without CJ McCollum and and Kingston Flemings in the lineup, the Hawks have been operating without a primary point guard. It was Dyson Daniels who stepped up with 10 assists last night to lead the Hawks from a playmaking perspective. Daniels himself believe it has been done by committee.

"I think everyone's stepped up," said Daniels of the point guard play without McCollum and Flemings. "Obviously, we don't have like Kingston or CJ, like a true point guard out there but we've just been pushing the pace. Everyone's getting touches, we've got guys that can play with the ball, guys that can play off the ball. So I think everyone's just filled those voids. Guys like RayJ (Dennis) have been really good coming in as well, playing, some good minutes for us. Corey (Kispert) coming as well, playing some really good minutes. Everyone's kind of stepped up, which has been good."

While Daniels starred in his own role, there was little doubt as to who the MVP was for the Hawks last night, and that was Jalen Johnson. Johnson scored 26 points on 10-of-15 shooting from the field and 3-of-4 from three:

As we looked at earlier, Johnson excelled in transition/off of Charlotte turnovers. The manner of which he was able to manoeuvre through bodies in transition to get to the rim looked effortless at times; he just looked in total command in the open court.

It hasn't been an easy season for Johnson shooting the three-pointer, shooting 22% on the season, and while it was encouraging to see Johnson hit 3-of-4 from, the poor percentages so far are't going to dissuade the Hawks and Johnson from shooting.

"I think he knows that we want him to keep shooting," said Snyder of Johnson. "The catch-and-shoots that he hit were quick decisions. I thought the thing that he did tonight where he was really impactful was he played without the ball. That's important for him and for us, particularly when people are game-planning for him, whether it was a matchup on the ball, his ability to do both, to get out in transition, to get to the foul line, to offensive rebound, all those things add up. I think it calms your nervous system a little bit, too. He knows how we feel about him. His shooting the ball is important for us, so it's good to see a couple go in, no question about it. I don't want to obsess about it either because there's lots of ways he can help us."

"I mean it happens," added Johnson of his three-point shooting. "Obviously I haven't shot the ball the way I wanted to but I put in the work. I'm not worried about the percentage or whatnot, I know my shot is going to fall. It was good to see a couple go in for sure. I'm going to keep shooting the shots that I work on and just continue to let them fall."

The Hawks move to 2-1 in the NBA Cup and are currently 2nd in the group, trailing the Boston Celtics who have played just two cup games (2-0). The Hawks and Celtics will face-off next Friday at the TD Garden, the winner practically guaranteed to advance from Group C. The Hawks are obviously motivated to win, but the mechanisms of the NBA Cup provide another incentive to win.

"I mean, obviously we want that money," said Jalen Johnson when asked how the Hawks prepare for a cup game. "So, I think it's as simple as that. Obviously we understand that the stakes are higher considering what's for the reward. So, I think we all young, we all like an extra prize and money. So, I think just upping our intensity, upping our focus."

The Hawks (10-6) are back in action on Sunday afternoon where they host the Detroit Pistons (10-5) at State Farm Arena.

#2852. State and federal battle over prediction markets regulation heats up after midterms

The turf war between states and the federal government is intensifying after Democrats secured control of Congress and key governorships on midterm elections night.

While it was widely expected Democrats would step up oversight of the emerging, multibillion-dollar industry if they won control of Congress, the actions after the elections have come from primarily Republican-led states.

At the same time, the Commodity Futures Trading Commission — the federal regulator for prediction markets —  has ramped up its oversight of "mention" markets, where traders speculate on what individuals will say during a speech or broadcast. It's a type of event contract the agency has said is susceptible to manipulation.

CNBC first reported on Tuesday that the CFTC placed a hold on the certification of markets related to President Donald Trump's words in mid-November. Kalshi said it was complying with the order, but CEO Tarek Mansour said at an Axios event on Tuesday that the company disagreed with the regulatory decision.

On Thursday, the Senate passed a bipartisan bill that would ban CFTC-registered entities from listing contracts involving terrorism, assassination, war or military operations. The measure also bars contracts that are "reasonably expected" to resolve based on an individual's death.

Utah Attorney General Derek Brown filed 14 criminal charges against Kalshi and its founders, Mansour and Luana Lopes Lara, on Oct. 28, asserting the company violated a state law that prohibits gambling.

Kalshi's founders are expected to appear in person at Utah's Third District Court on Dec. 4, according to court documents.

"This is a crazy overreach; Utah is clearly playing politics. We look forward to putting a stop to this in court, just like we did in Arizona," said Kalshi's spokesperson Dani Lever.

In a separate, civil complaint on Oct. 20, Utah also named institutional market maker Susquehanna International Group as a defendant, along with Kalshi.

Though Arizona Attorney General Kris Mayes filed a criminal complaint against Kalshi in March, a federal judge blocked the case from proceeding in April.

Louisiana's Republican attorney general also filed a complaint to ban multiple exchanges from operating in the state on Nov. 12. A state judge ordered a temporary restraining order against the prediction market companies that same day.

"Who would have thought that these red states, that were reluctant to jump into the litigation arena, ended up having the most boldest and most innovative solutions," said gambling attorney Daniel Wallach, noting that Louisiana and Utah's latest lawsuits were led by Republican attorneys general.

In response, the CFTC sued Louisiana on Nov. 13. Separately, the federal agency filed suit against Arkansas after that state pursued civil enforcement against Kalshi.

The CFTC has sued 12 states as it tries to defend what it views as its exclusive jurisdiction to regulate prediction markets.

Meanwhile, the CFTC may soon add commissioners. President Donald Trump sent bipartisan nominations to the U.S. Senate on Nov. 12 to fill two of the four open seats on the commission. Chairman Michael Selig has been the only member since his nomination was approved by the Senate in December 2025.

Nathan Anonick, a Republican, and Matthew Rowland, a Democrat, appeared before the Senate Agriculture Committee on Tuesday. Multiple senators questioned them on the issue of prediction markets. Both demurred when asked whether they viewed sports-related event contracts as gambling.

The CFTC's position faced another setback in a complicated decision by the Fourth U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals this week. The court ruled 2-1 that Maryland can enforce its state gambling law against Kalshi, though the judges disagreed on the reasoning.

"Judges can't agree with each other on why we're wrong," Kalshi spokesperson Elisabeth Diana said in a statement about the decision. "This splintered decision, including three separate contradictory opinions, demonstrates there is no unified coherent legal argument against prediction markets."

Overall, prediction markets have now seen three losses and one win at the circuit court level. It's widely expected the question of whether states or the federal government can regulate sports-related event contracts will be decided by the Supreme Court.

Kalshi on Nov. 12  ended its advisory relationship with Donald Trump Jr. The prediction platform pointed to his venture capital firm 1789 Capital having a stake in rival platform Polymarket.

Though 1789 Capital announced its holding in Polymarket in August, Kalshi and the president's son ended their relationship after Democrats secured a majority in the House and Senate. Democratic lawmakers are expected to probe companies with relationships to the Trump family.

Nine Democratic senators, led by Adam Schiff, D-California, on Monday sent a letter to the CFTC asking it to reverse a directive to platforms not to geofence their offerings when they receive unfavorable court rulings stopping exchanges from operating in certain states.

Geofencing involves the use of location technology to block users in certain jurisdictions from participating in events contracts.

"Your unilateral decision directly conflicts with active court orders, undermines state and tribal law, and must be reversed," the senators wrote.

Meanwhile, Democratic Rep. Ro Khanna, D-California, has called for Congress to ban sports-related prediction markets entirely.

"Kalshi, a $40B company, lets 18 year olds bet on sports. They call it a 'prediction market.' It's gambling," he wrote in a post on X on Wednesday. "Investigate and ban sports prediction markets."

Disclosure: CNBC and Kalshi have a commercial relationship that includes customer acquisition and a minority investment.

#2851. Eagles place Zack Baun, Dontayvion Wicks on IR in roster flurry

Eagles place Zack Baun, Dontayvion Wicks on IR in roster flurry originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

The Eagles on Saturday afternoon placed LB Zack Baun (back) and WR Dontayvion Wicks (ankle/foot) on Injured Reserve in a flurry of roster moves.

They will miss at least the next four games.

Baun missed all week of practice and was ruled out on Friday. Without him, the Eagles will turn to Jihaad Campbell and Jeremiah Trotter Jr. as starters. Before Campbell missed a couple games with injuries of his own, those two split reps next to Baun. This will give the Eagles an extended look at both of those young 'backers.

Baun, 29, was playing at a very high level again this season. The two-time Pro Bowler had 65 tackles, 2 sacks, 8 TFLs, 5 QB hits and 5 pass breakups in 8 games this season.

Wicks, 25, missed the three games before the bye week with an ankle injury but was able to return to practice this week. He then was ruled out for this game on Friday as the foot injury was added to the report. At least DeVonta Smith (hamstring) will return this weekend for the Birds.

In addition to placing those two players on IR, the Eagles made the following roster moves on Saturday:

• RB Will Shipley (foot) was activated off Injured Reserve, where he had been since Oct. 16. Shipley was able to return to practice this week. He joins Saquon Barkley and Jaydon Blue as the running backs on the roster.

Expect Shipley to slot in between Barkley and Blue in the pecking order. He'll also take a significant role on special teams. Shipley will retake his role as a kick returner.

• Both DT Gabe Hall and LB Anfernee Orji were elevated for the Steelers game.

Hall will be the fifth defensive tackle after the Eagles traded Jordan Davis ahead of the deadline. He's behind Jalen Carter, Moro Ojomo, Byron Young and Ty Robinson. Sunday will be Hall's 2026 debut; he played in two games in 2025.

Orji first joined the Eagles' practice squad in early October. This is his second stint with the Eagles. He hasn't played in a regular season game since 2024 with the Saints.

#2850. Kentucky’s Losing Streak Hits Three

Kentucky's losing streak hits three. For the first two months of the season, the Kentucky Wildcats did everything right. They started 7-1 and were ranked sixth in the playoff rankings, their highest ever. Since then, pretty much everything that can go wrong has. Even with back-to-back losses to Tennessee and then to Florida, the Cats, ranked 16th, still had a chance if they got some help. As it happened, by the time they took the field Saturday night, three teams in front of them had lost. Unfortunately, with everything on the line, they couldn't finish the job. Let's break it down as Kentucky's losing streak hits three after falling to Missouri 28-14.

Will Stein's club had one of their best drives in recent memory to start the game. Going 55 yards in ten plays, Kenny Mnchey led the Cats deep into Tigers' territory. CJ Baxter's huge second effort scored an apparent touchdown. However, the officials blew him down short of the goal line, which on replay looked like a bad call.

The next play, UK was called for illegal motion, moving them back to the six. On third down, a pass a little high to Willie Rodriguez couldn't be hauled in, and the Cats settled for three. It was going to be that kind of night. Kentucky's next possession also ended without a score as Rodriguez dropped a possible touchdown pass on fourth down. Two trips to the red zone resulted in three points. You aren't going to win like that.

One player who had no trouble finding the end zone was Tigers' quarterback Austin Simmons. Tied for the SEC lead in passing touchdowns with 21, Simmons added two more in the first half. The first came to Donovan Olugbode, and the second to Jude James. It looked like UK's Terhyon Nichols was going to get the interception. However, he mistimed his jump, and Missouri got the score. It was one of those nights as Kentucky's losing streak goes to three.

Luckily for the Cats, Nichols got some revenge late in the half as he picked off Simmons to keep the deficit to only 14-3 at the half.

Simmons picked up right where he left off in the second half. Throwing two more touchdowns, the lead quickly bloomed to 28-6.

For the game, he threw for 314 yards and four scores, with the only blemish coming on the interception.

On the other side of the ball, Minchey's recent struggles continued. He finished with 268 yards through the air. However, he was picked off twice, and the Tigers had two more called back due to penalties.

Despite the problems, Kentucky still had chances. With Baxter and Jason Patterson out due to injury, the Cats needed big plays from Jovantae Barnes. Not only did he score on a three-yard run, but he hauled in the two-point conversion, and it was 28-14. The defense got a stop, but Barnes fumbled on the next drive. An interception followed, and it was game over as Kentucky's losing streak hit three as they lost to Missouri 28-14.

With the loss, Kentucky drops to 7-4 on the season and finishes 5-4 in league play. Next Saturday, they'll host a red-hot Louisville Cardinals club looking to make it three in a row in the "Battle For The Governor's Cup." With other teams losing, we'll see how this affects the Wildcats' bowl destination.

The win moves Eli Drinkwitz's Tigers to 7-4 on the year and 4-4 in the SEC. They will wrap up the regular season next week when they host Oklahoma before waiting the hear their bowl spot.

In a game full of negatives, one bright spot was the play of standout freshman receiver Kenny Darby. With eight catches for 100 yards, he broke Barion Brown's single-season records for catches and yards by a freshman.

Kentucky got into the Red Zone seven times against Missouri and only came away with 14 points. As we've seen in every game since the bye week, turnovers and penalties have undone them. It's two straight weeks the win was there only to fall short.

When the calendar turned to November, many in the BBN were thinking of a "November to Remember." Instead, it's been one to forget. They've got one more chance next week against Louisville. However, lose that one and go 0-4 down the stretch is a tough pill to swallow and a whole lot of "what might have been."

On a day, the Wildcats outgained the Tigers 446-391; miscues and mistakes did them in. That's for checking out our recap as Kentucky's losing streak hits three with a 28-14 loss to Missouri.

Wake Forest Takes Apart Georgia Tech, 50-29

#2849. Tech outages are fueling a new doomsday economy

Americans are shopping like there's no tomorrow.

A spate of high-profile technology outages that grounded planes, halted email access and slowed bank transactions have left many people worried about artificial intelligence-driven cyberattacks and the state of the U.S. energy grid.

Government officials have dismissed rumors and speculation circulating online that there could be a nefarious culprit for the series of malfunctions. Despite those assurances, consumers like Sabrina Cepeda are readying for a larger-scale technology blackout or other emergency event.

In recent days, the Houston resident has purchased propane and lighter fluid for cooking and heating equipment that can be used without electricity. The mother of three also replenished her backup stockpiles of non-perishable foods, hygiene products and allergy medicine in the event that her family is homebound for several days.

"I have a way to keep my family fed, clean, hydrated, warm and safe," said Cepeda, 39. "You want to have these things and not need them, rather than need them and not have them."

The doomsday economy is catching a bid. Wall Street and retailers are taking note.

At least half a dozen major technology outages took place in the U.S. between Nov. 12 and Nov. 17. In that time frame, spending at brands specializing in emergency products spiked about 400% compared with the same period a year prior, according to card data analyzed exclusively for CNBC by Consumer Edge.

At Ready America, which sells products like emergency survival kits, prepackaged water and high-calorie food bars, sales through e-commerce platforms have seen a roughly five-fold increase in recent days. In addition, corporate customers who keep the Vista, California-based company's supplies in their offices in case of emergency have called in to order more.

The company has rarely seen this much demand since the Covid pandemic sent Americans into lockdown more than six years ago, said Ready America President Trevyn Reese. As a result, Ready America asked its suppliers to scale up production. The family-owned business has faced a shortage of water purification tablets, and redirected shoppers to alternative offerings like prepackaged water pouches.

"With everything going on in the news right now, we've seen a large spike of people wanting to get prepared," said Reese.

While the string of outages brought power-related concerns to a boiling point, data shows that worries had been bubbling under the surface for weeks.

Shoppers began significantly ramping up buying from emergency preparedness brands late last month, according to Consumer Edge. Around that time, Fox News reported that the Pentagon was preparing U.S. military bases to operate for at least 90 days without external support. Exacerbating those fears was the ongoing war with Iran that President Donald Trump signaled could ramp back up.

Firearm trade group NSSF said that gun sales rose about 8% in October from the same month a year prior. Retailers and gun manufacturers have anecdotally reported that demand has remained elevated into November, which NSSF said can be attributed to a combination of uncertainty tied to the midterm election and the recent outages.

"If people are investing in firearms and ammunition, they're telling you very directly, with their wallet, what their priority is," said Mark Oliva, NSSF's managing director of public affairs. "You're literally investing in a product that's going to be able to protect your life and protect the lives of your family."

Now, the doomsday talk has begun making its way to Wall Street.

Mizuho told clients on Monday that consumer worries about the outages — coupled with what the bank called "apocalyptic concerns" swirling on social media — drove shoppers to stock up on emergency supplies at Costco. As a result, Mizuho said it expects a key measure of non-fuel sales growth for Costco in November to rise above 10% for the first time since 2022.

And RBC said so-called doomsday prepping has had a "real impact" on buying behavior around the globe over recent weeks. Still, the Canadian bank reported that the trend has so far boosted sales for only a small basket of items, such as shelf-stable proteins, water purification products and dry-pantry foods.

Clients of Richard McWhorter, a private wealth advisor at SRM, have been rushing to Costco to buy goods in bulk and splurging for power generators. McWhorter said he's also fielded questions about how to prepare for a situation in which the financial system goes down.

"I've never seen anything like this in my life," McWhorter said. "There's the financial side of what I do, and then there's the psychological side. And the past two weeks have been much more psychological."

Metal dealer American Hartford Gold reported an uptick in customer inquiries this week. Company president Max Baecker said he heard from some customers that their interest in products like gold — long considered a safe-haven asset during times of turmoil — was driven by newfound concern about being overly reliant on digital financial networks following a glitch at JPMorgan Chase that affected customer transactions for several hours.

Vice President JD Vance abruptly canceled his appearance at a New York Times summit on Wednesday to attend meetings about the technology outages. A day prior, Jay Clayton, the U.S. director of national intelligence, told CNBC that the recent outages were not a cyberattack or a coordinated effort by a foreign nation.

Still, Sen. Mark Warner, D-Va., told CNBC on Wednesday that people were "freaked out" by the outages. Warner, who acts as the vice chairman of the Senate Intelligence Committee, said that the U.S. was "lucky" to have not yet seen a major AI-generated cyberattack or crisis due to AI agents going rogue.

Investors may begin pushing companies to accelerate their cybersecurity spending out of an abundance of caution around rogue AI agents following the outages, according to Macro Hive CEO Bilal Hafeez. Such a situation would be reminiscent of the antivirus software push seen during the dot-com boom, he said.

The Global X Cybersecurity ETF (BUG) has climbed around 3% over the past two weeks, bucking the downtrend seen among AI stocks and the broader technology trade. The Nasdaq Composite on Friday fell into negative territory for 2026, putting the tech-heavy index on track for its first losing year since 2022.

To be sure, not everyone on social media has bought into the idea that they should be prepping for a doomsday scenario. In satirical videos posted on TikTok, users joked that they were gearing up by placing orders in the Starbucks drive-thru or purchasing significant quantities of alcohol.

Still, the topic appears to be on the minds of a growing number of Americans. U.S. Google searches for the term "prepping" have more than doubled when comparing the past month with the same period a year prior. On YouTube, queries for videos about "survivalism," a term describing the practice of preparing for emergencies, have soared by more than 5,000% over the same time frame.

Cepeda, the Houston mom, sees emergency prep efforts as a way to be safe rather than sorry. She's previously tapped into backup food stashes during extreme weather events like Hurricane Harvey in 2017 and Winter Storm Uri in 2021.

But this year is the first time that she's felt she needed to be prepared for a potential emergency caused by technology outages, she said. Cepeda and her husband have ensured they have enough cash on hand and fuel in their cars if there's a broader hit to the U.S. power grid, she said.

"We rely so much on technology," Cepeda said. "You need to be able to do these things the more traditional, old-fashioned way."

#2848. The Lakers may soon experiment with a very interesting lineup

The benefit of having two elite ballhandlers is that one of them should always be on the floor. For the Lakers, when both Luka Dončić and Austin Reaves have been healthy this season, they have been staggered so that one of them is always playing.

But head coach JJ Redick and his staff are open to the idea of mixing things up where that's not the case in the future.

Ahead of the team's recent game against the Jazz, Redick was asked about Grimes' role this season and whether the team might keep him in the starting lineup even when Reaves returns from injury to help him keep his rhythm. While Redick was noncommittal on a starting lineup change, he did offer insight into another way the Lakers are looking to help the non-Luka and Reaves players find a rhythm.

"We're kind of talking about as a staff," Redick said, "but we just haven't had a stretch of time, a stretch of games to do this, is trying to find more minutes with, when we're healthy, with no Luka, no AR and and see if that can sort of get those guys going on their own. So then, second half when we kind of make those decisions in the fourth quarter about who we want to finish the game with, those guys are in a rhythm.

"I think that's probably what we're focused on once we're fully healthy rather than long term starting lineups because, again, I think with this group and the way this group, it's just an egoless group. We're going to be able to be flexible with the starting lineup and flexible with matchups."

There's not a great deal of harm in experimenting, even if it's an idea that feels a little far-fetched. The Lakers have struggled in the non-Luka minutes with Austin out, so I'm not sure it makes sense to go to those minutes when they are healthy.

The idea might make sense on paper. It gives other players more freedom. But it comes at the risk of not having elite players on the court.

We'll see whether Redick and his staff ever implement this idea. It feels like a pretty risky proposition. Is it worth potentially losing a couple games to try out this theory, especially if it doesn't work?

#2847. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2846. Back at 100%, DeVonta Smith defends playing in Panthers game

Back at 100%, DeVonta Smith defends playing in Panthers game originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

DeVonta Smith was back at practice on Wednesday afternoon as the Eagles returned from their Week 10 bye. The 28-year-old was a limited participant but said he's 100% after missing the last three games with a hamstring injury.

Of course, it's probably too little, too late.

The Eagles have lost seven straight games and, at 2-7, find themselves in the basement of the NFC East. Smith took over as the Eagles' WR1 in 2026 but has played in just four of nine games this season.

But Smith on Wednesday defended the decision to play against the Panthers back in Week 6. If you remember, he missed Weeks 4 and 5 with a hamstring injury and then ended up aggravating it against the Panthers and missed the next three games.

Smith claims he was 100% healthy going into that Panthers game, but still aggravated the injury.

"I know someone asked Nick (Sirianni) about shutting me down during the season," Smith said. "I didn't bust my ass this whole summer, I didn't continue to get up at 4:30 in the morning every day, to come out here and not play. My focus has been to getting where I was supposed to be.

"I felt 100% that game. I feel 100% now. Like I say, if something happens, it's out of my control because I know I do everything I can do for my body to make sure I'm in tip-top shape."

Smith was on a pitch count in that Panthers game and ended up playing 42 snaps, which he said was right on target. But he clearly tweaked the hamstring during that game and missed the three division losses against the Cowboys, Commanders and Giants.

In hindsight, the Eagles probably could have waited for Smith to return for those crucial NFC East matchups. Maybe it would have preserved him and the season, but maybe it wouldn't have mattered.

Smith said he has given up on being frustrated about the injury.

"I do everything I can do," Smith said. "I've been doing everything I can do. That's why, myself, I kind of got to the point where I was like, 'Maybe this is what God wanted' for whatever reason it is. Because I did everything I can do and if it happens, then it's out of my control. There's nothing I can do about it."

The one bright spot of Smith and Dontayvion Wicks missing the last three games is that rookie Makai Lemon made the most of his extra opportunities. The first-round pick had the three best games of his young career.

Lemon had 7 for 67 and a touchdown against the Cowboys, 6 for 57 against the Commanders and 6 for 61 against the Giants. Smith has spent his time off the field trying to help his young teammates as much as possible and was impressed by those performances from Lemon.

"The biggest thing is just going out there and knowing what to do," Smith said. "I think he's settled down a lot into knowing what he has to do and things like that. He's going out there, he's thinking less, he's playing a lot faster and I think that's the biggest thing. You can go out there and play fast and play free, you're going to be alright."

The last time we saw Smith in Week 6, the Eagles' starting quarterback was Tanner McKee. So Sunday against the Steelers will be his first game with Joe Milton under center.

Smith said he is using this week to learn as much as he can about the way Milton operates. He also admitted that Milton is still learning the offense.

This is Smith's six NFL season and he has been to the playoffs in the previous five. That streak is very likely going to end in 2026 as the Eagles enter the second half of their season with a 2-7 record.

Smith admitted has been a frustrating season from a team perspective.

"But at the end of the day, it's not over," Smith said. "Especially in this division. I feel like there's still a chance in our division right now but we can only take it one week at a time so focusing on this week. Going out there and getting a win would be the biggest thing right now. Getting back on track."

#2845. Trump says there could be a 'positive spin' from the latest FBI database hack

President Donald Trump suggested Saturday that there could be a bright spot in the latest incursion into the FBI computer database.

The president confirmed in an interview with Fox News that a system has been breached that houses information used by law enforcement at all levels to share criminal justice information.

The hackers accessed the National Criminal Information Center (NCIC), a database that includes FBI fingerprint records, wanted persons, violent felons, missing persons and related information, two people familiar with the matter told MS NOW. The database is restricted to law enforcement use but not classified and it is unclear what data was extracted.

CNN had first reported on the suspected hack, citing sources familiar with the issue.

In a wide-ranging interview on Fox, Trump said there could be upside from the news.

"Maybe we'll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place," he said. "I always like to put things on a positive spin."

This would be the second recent intrusion into the FBI system; the previous one came in September, when a group known as "ShinyHunters" broke into an FBI jobs website. The result compromised data on current and former FBI employees and shut down part of the agency's operations until the issue was resolved.

Trump said it's possible there could be information gleaned from the latest hack.

"I wonder who's doing it, because wouldn't it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?" he said. "People should hear about it, because it actually put our country in danger, and so maybe we'll learn things that we wouldn't have learned otherwise."

The FBI did not immediately respond to a CNBC request for comment.

#2844. Anthropic’s record IPO collides with tech’s worst week on Wall Street since 2020

Startup investor Ben Narasin is so jazzed about Anthropic's blockbuster IPO that he's planning a party later this month at his Silicon Valley home. Sake, vodka and Krug Champagne will be served. Each attendee will be given two jars of caviar.

"This is a celebration of the most material, significant, and mind-blowingly awesome event that has ever happened in venture capital," Narasin, founding partner of Tenacity Venture Capital, told CNBC in an interview, adding that he didn't even invest in the company.

Anthropic raised a record $93.5 billion in its IPO and an additional $26 billion from tech giants that bought at the offer price. The stock gained 17% in its debut on Thursday, lifting Anthropic's market cap to $1.8 trillion and providing a much-needed windfall for Silicon Valley's startup ecosystem.

It was quite the split-screen moment on Wall Street, which spent the week in an AI-driven tailspin. Led by Nvidia's 23% plunge, which marked the stock's sharpest weekly pullback in 18 years, the Nasdaq slid 10% over the past five trading days in its worst week since the early days of Covid in 2020.

Investors and analysts spent the week debating whether Anthropic will trade alongside AI hardware, as it's reliant on advanced chips, memory and data centers, or whether it will stand alone as the first public pure-play AI developer and a company that's achieved unrivaled growth in less than six years of existence.

That question will likely persist for some time, but Friday's market action showed that Wall Street is skeptical of Anthropic's ability to grow into its lofty valuation. At week's end, the Claude creator's $1.6 trillion fully diluted market cap put it behind just six U.S. companies: Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet, Nvidia, Amazon and SpaceX.

With the tech market in turmoil due to rising interest rates, raging anti-AI sentiment and Nvidia's C-suite crisis, Anthropic's IPO was part exuberance, part relief for an industry hungry for exits.

After venture-backed IPOs yielded $73 billion in proceeds in 2021, the amount raised in the next four years totaled just $39 billion, according to data from University of Florida emeritus professor Jay Ritter. In 2021, 126 technology companies went public, compared with 34 in 2025.

Meanwhile, the private market has continued to balloon, with companies raising ever-larger sums at sky-high valuations without the pressures of quarterly earnings. Many startups that had hoped to go public early in the decade fell out of favor in the AI boom. They experienced hefty job cuts, down rounds and acquisitions for pennies on the dollar.

"There was a time in Silicon Valley not that long ago where the prevailing wisdom was the default path should be avoiding going public for as long as possible," said Somesh Dash, a general partner at venture firm IVP. The view was, "it's too long, it's too hard, it's not worth it," he said.

Anthropic is very young compared to some of those companies, but it was a huge collector of private capital. Investors poured $141 billion into the AI lab as of September, according to Anthropic's prospectus.

For a few Silicon Valley firms that contributed to those funding rounds, the returns are unlike anything they've ever seen. At the IPO price, Menlo Ventures and Lightspeed Venture Partners each owned about $25 billion worth of Anthropic stock. Bessemer Venture Partners owned a stake worth roughly $10 billion, partner Byron Deeter said.

Matt Murphy, a partner at Menlo, told CNBC's David Faber on Thursday that his firm has more than five limited partners "that we delivered over $1 billion of returns to."

"At the end of the day, that's really our job, is to get dollars back into foundations and endowments," Murphy said. "There's a lot of pride in being able to do that."

Bessemer's Deeter was also jubilant on Thursday, posting on X that, in addition to being "the largest bet in Bessemer's history," the IPO "is a punctuation mark in the history of both humanity and computation."

That's quite a change from a few years earlier, when Anthropic was hardly generating revenue, and success was far from guaranteed. In 2023, Anthropic's Claude 2 was struggling to keep pace with models from Google and OpenAI. In April 2024, investors at Bessemer wrote in a memo that if Anthropic missed its own targets, there was "a good chance that we lose all or part of our money."

Guru Chahal at Lightspeed told CNBC that Anthropic was clearly the team taking a more measured approach to AI. The company looked beyond Nvidia's dominant AI chips, choosing to use alternatives from Amazon and Google as well. Anthropic said it generated $1.72 in revenue per dollar spent on computing power in the third quarter, up from 68 cents a year earlier.

"It is true that it required lots of capital, but when you dig in and you realize that capital is going toward compute, and this is the team that's positioned to use that compute in the most effective way, then we could connect those dots," Chahal said. He added that those metrics showed it was "going to be a very high-quality business."

Anthropic isn't the only big win for VCs this year.

Five months before Anthropic's Nasdaq debut, SpaceX hit the market in what, until this week, was by far the largest IPO in history. SpaceX, which Elon Musk founded in 2002, produced outsized returns for firms including Sequoia and Andreessen Horowitz.

SpaceX and Anthropic alone will make 2026 a monster year for the venture industry, easily exceeding any other year in IPO proceeds. And investors in OpenAI hope appetite remains strong for trillion-dollar companies next year, as the ChatGPT maker is looking to 2027 for a potential offering.

IVP's Dash said that in his AI portfolio, "a lot of companies are starting IPO prep" even if it's only "year four, year five, year six since inception." He hopes to see at least half a dozen companies go public at valuations north of $10 billion each in the second half of 2027.

Jensen Huang's sudden departure from Nvidia has left the chip giant without a clear leader at a time when the company already faced heightened concerns about a slowdown in spending. Nvidia is a key investor in Anthropic and OpenAI and a critical supplier and financing partner for the entire AI industry, which is getting hammered across the board.

In January, Democrats will take control of the House and Senate after this month's midterm elections, which included the passage of numerous ballot measures that could stymie data center development. Anthropic investor Brad Gerstner predicted after the election that market dynamics would push Anthropic to price toward the lower end of its range.

Narasin said he wasn't even sure the company would get that far.

"I was concerned they'd delay," Narasin said.

That makes the outcome that much more impressive, he said, after Anthropic priced above its expected range and upsized the offering.

"This will be bedtime stories for years for future VCs," Narasin said. "There was a company that was created, backed by angels and later venture" that in a few short years "became the single biggest IPO of all history."

— CNBC's Ari Levy contributed to this report.

#2843. James Harden's 51-point night moves him past Kobe Bryant on 50+ point game list

James Harden will go into the Hall of Fame one day, remembered as one of the great scorers the game has ever seen.

As evidence, we present Friday night, when he dropped 51 on the Indiana Pacers in a Cavaliers win — the 26th 50+ point game of Harden's career, moving him past Kobe Bryant and into third all-time in 50+ point games (Michael Jordan at 31 and Wilt Chamberlain at a laughable 118 are ahead of him).

"It's a different role for me. My usage rate isn't extremely high every game, we have somebody for that," Harden said of his scoring outburst postgame, via the Associated Press. "So mine is kind of scoring when I need to, but then distributing the ball. My number is called and you need to go out there and put the shots up. Ok, I can still do it. I knew I can do it, but it's just when you don't do something consistently and then it goes away a little bit or you just don't know if you got it or not."

He's still got it, he was 15-of-26 from the field and knocked down seven 3-pointers.

Cleveland has found a bit of a groove, having won 3 of 4 and is now 2-1 in East Group B of the NBA Cup. The Cavaliers still have a chance of making the knockout round of Cup play, but they need a convincing win over the Knicks in Madison Square Garden next Wednesday.

#2842. Rams WR Puka Nacua gets knee surgery, likely out for season

Rams WR Puka Nacua gets knee surgery, likely out for season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

All-Pro receiver Puka Nacua is likely to miss the rest of the season after undergoing knee surgery, Los Angeles Rams coach Sean McVay said Friday.

Nacua injured ligaments in his knee while carrying the ball on the final offensive snap of the Rams' win over the Washington Commanders on Nov. 8.

McVay had expressed optimism last week that Nacua could return to the field this season, but he said the star wideout decided this week that he should have knee surgery, which was performed Tuesday.

Nacua received a three-game suspension from the NFL for violating its personal conduct policies on Thursday. His suspension will expire during his mandated stint on injured reserve, but the knee surgery will keep him out much longer.

McVay didn't rule out a return by Nacua during the playoffs, but he wouldn't speculate that far ahead.

Nacua has been one of the most productive receivers in NFL history during his first four seasons and has 70 catches for 715 yards and four touchdowns while playing in just seven games this year. He is in the final year of his rookie contract and is eligible to sign a large new deal.

The Rams (6-4), currently outside of the playoff picture, will be without their top two pass-catchers when they host the Green Bay Packers on Wednesday night. Davante Adams also isn't ready to return from his hamstring injury, but McVay is optimistic he can play the following week.

#2841. Why one NBA GM reportedly shied away from trading up to draft Yaxel Lendeborg

Why one NBA GM reportedly shied away from trading up to draft Yaxel Lendeborg originally appeared on NBC Sports Bay Area

A Western Conference general manager had Yaxel Lendeborg fifth on his 2026 NBA Draft board, considered trading into the top 10 to take him and then shied away, according to ESPN's Tim MacMahon.

The executive was not identified, and his biggest concern was Lendeborg's age.

"He has to be really good right away or it's a problem," the GM told MacMahon who also reported the unnamed exec's hesitancy to draft Lendeborg was "in part due to worries about how the rookie's 'goofy' personality would fit in his team's locker room."

Lendeborg turned 24 before making his NBA debut, which was the "biggest knock" on him among NBA execs, MacMahon reported. The last lottery pick that old was Chris Duarte, the 13th pick in 2021, who averaged 13.1 points as a rookie and was out of the league after four seasons. Lendeborg has addressed the age talk himself, saying that winning Rookie of the Year would come with a "he's 24" caveat no matter what.

So far, the Warriors rookie has not given anyone a reason to worry. The No. 11 pick ranks second among rookies in scoring (19.7 points) and rebounding (7.3) and fifth in assists (3.9) through 16 games, with MacMahon calling him the likely steal of the draft.

Another West executive told MacMahon it is hard to rule him out as the best player in the class, and an Eastern Conference executive told them he could not have predicted Lendeborg would be this good.

His shooting has helped. MacMahon noted that Lendeborg has improved as a 3-point shooter at every stop, from 33.3 percent in his first season at UAB to 37.2 percent at Michigan and 37.8 percent in the NBA, even with a slump since Steph Curry's injury made him the main focus of opposing scouting reports. Curry has an oblique abdominal tear and was set to be re-evaluated in two weeks.

For the GM who passed, the early verdict is clear: Lendeborg has been really good right away.

The Eagles are getting some key offensive reinforcements as they return from their Week 10 bye week.

Limited: DeVonta Smith (hamstring), Michael Carter (abdomen), Britain Covey (knee), Jonathan Jones (hamstring), Will Shipley (foot), Nolan Smith (illness), Dontayvion Wicks (ankle)

Full: Saquon Barkley (hamstring), Jihaad Campbell (hamstring/quad), Jonathan Greenard (ankle), Tanner McKee (rib), Smael Mondon (ankle), Drew Mukuba (knee)

The only running backs on the 53-man roster right now are Saquon Barkley and Jaydon Blue. Shipley was getting reps as a kick returner during the early portion of practice.

#2839. Saquon Barkley healthier after bye, hoping to salvage his season

Saquon Barkley healthier after bye, hoping to salvage his season originally appeared on NBC Sports Philadelphia

Before the bye week, Saquon Barkley was battling through hamstring and back injuries for a few games.

As the Eagles get ready for the second half of their season, Barkley is definitely feeling healthier. But it doesn't sound like he's 100 percent, nor will he be.

"I wish the bye week was like a magic pill and everything just vanishes," Barkley said. "But that's only in la-la land, I guess. But it definitely did help."

Barkley, 29, has had a disappointing season. Injuries have limited him, but he has just 69 rushing attempts for 296 yards and 3 touchdowns. It's obviously not all his fault, of course. Barkley is playing behind a struggling offensive line in a dysfunctional offense.

But he also doesn't think he is personally playing to his capabilities.

"I think the biggest thing you could do in a bye week is go back and watch yourself on film," Barkley said. "Watch other football, watch other games and see how well other backs are doing. And just be honest with yourself. If I'm being honest with myself, the last couple weeks, for whatever reason, I don't like the way I was playing and what I was putting out on the field.

"The way I go about changing that is the only way I know how to, just put my head down and go to work, just continue to be consistent. But it's got to look different. It's got to look different than what it was in '24 and '25 because every season is going to be different challenges. Felt really good this week to be able to have a full week, go out there and practice and put the work in. And hopefully I see the reward on Sunday."

The last two games for Barkley haven't gone well. Against the Commanders, he had 13 carries for 30 yards and against the Giants, he had 13 carries for 22 yards. His yards-per-carry average dropped from 5.6 to 4.3 on the season after those two losses.

So Barkley and his teammates happily welcomed a bye week on the heels of a seven-game losing streak.

"I think it was helpful for everyone," Barkley said. "From staff to everyone that works in this building to players. Sometimes when a season is not going the way you wanted it to go and you're facing a lot of adversity, that week off is really important. More so for just the mind also. Obviously, the body. It's good for the body. But just the mind and getting your mind somewhere else a little bit. And then regroup and come back ready to work."

As the Eagles look toward the last eight games of the season, they are in the basement of the NFC East with a 2-7 record. They are not technically dead just yet, but it would take a mini miracle for them to pull themselves out of this.

But that's easy to say on the outside. For the guys in the locker room, they have to keep fighting even after they eventually get eliminated. That starts with Sunday against a Steelers team that ranks third in the NFL in rushing defense.

"One game at a time," Barkley said. "I said it already, I'm going to say it again. We put ourselves in this situation. Only way we're going to get out of it is by digging ourselves out. I still believe we have the men and women in this locker room and in this building to get it done. … Technically, we're still in the division and obviously has that crossed my mind? Of course. But can't even take it there.

"Just have to be focused on one game at at time and staying present and taking advantage of the opportunities and this opportunity we have coming up against a really good team who's trying to get back on track and definitely didn't like the way that they played on national television."

#2838. Can Cameron Carr and Adou Thiero stay in the Lakers’ rotation?

Did I jinx the Lakers with my headline on the latest mailbag? At the very least, it wasn't a great look on my part.

My defense is that the Lakers were overdue for a loss and the energy and emotions in Tuesday's win over the Hornets eventually came back to bite them. They haven't been convincing in their current homestand and can certainly use a return from Austin Reaves, but things could be worse.

So, let's dive into your questions for this week.

We got a peek at the answer to this when the Lakers were in Milwaukee as head coach JJ Redick and the team were playing an 11-man rotation prior to Austin Reaves' injury.

That feels like stretching things a bit much unless it's at the end of a stretch of three games in four days or a long road trip. It's more likely the team settles on a 10-man rotation once Reaves returns, with either Cameron Carr or Adou Thiero as the swing player. When they went to 11 guys, both youngsters played.

I think the Lakers will maintain a 10-man rotation and keep Austin's minutes low once he returns. Right now, I wouldn't be surprised if Carr continues to get minutes after his strong showing against the Kings.

But as Redick recently stated, too, it's going to be very rare that the team has every player healthy and available for any significant stretch. Having a deep team allows you to absorb those injuries and continue to win as much as it allows you to extend the rotation.

The Lakers have had Luka and Austin on the floor together for 3/4 of a game in the last seven contests and are 6-1 in that span. That's because of the depth.

The next step in the transition will be the final step. It's just a matter of whether it will include the Buss family or not.

Based on the timeline for the sale from the Buss family to Mark Walter, the NBA finalizing the terms of the deal should come in the next month or two. However, the Buss family is set for a court date on Dec. 8. The only way it feels like they'd be included in the transition from Walter to Josh Kusher and Bob Iger is if the siblings come to an agreement at or before that date.

The more likely path is that the majority of the team is sold and the Buss family share is potentially included at a later date.

When it comes to any other positions being filled, that likely won't come until the sale is finalized. The hiring of Eric Amsler as assistant general manager filled the biggest opening.

Once the sale is official, it's really a blank slate for what positions to hire. The team should continue to look to expand out the analytics and scouting departments. But there isn't a limit to how many people they can hire and they absolutely take advantage of that.

Stephen A. Smith was at the forefront of ruining sports media and no Laker should ever acknowledge him.

#2837. Tom Brady, after selling Birmingham City stake, buys piece of Chelsea

Tom Brady’s exit from the English soccer business was only temporary.

#2836. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2835. Ottawa Senators' GM Scouts Saturday's Game Between Penguins, Predators

It's not too often you see a general manager of an NHL team that is not even one of the two teams in the building take in a game during the month of November.

But that's exactly what one GM did during Saturday's matchup between the Pittsburgh Penguins and Nashville Predators.

Ottawa Senators' GM Steve Staios was in the press box at Bridgestone Arena in Nashville to watch the Penguins take on the Predators. Staios, 53, was hired as the interim GM of the Sens in the summer of 2023 and has been a permanent fixture in that role ever since.

And, given some recent trade speculation, it's not entirely a surprise - even if it's a pretty big statement for a GM to show up to a game like this - that Staios was in attendance.

Multiple reports in recent days, including from NHL Insiders Pierre LeBrun and Elliotte Friedman, have indicated that the Senators are interested in Penguins' veteran wingers Rickard Rakell and Bryan Rust. Rust is currently on injured reserve with a lower-body injury, but Rakell, 33, put together a pretty good performance in the game despite not registering any points.

Rakell is tied for the Penguins' team lead in goals with eight despite having no goals in his first nine games of the season. He has eight goals and 11 points in his last 13 games. Rust, 34, has three goals and five points in seven games this season and is expected back from injured reserve sometime in mid-December.

Despite Ottawa's hot start - they lead the competitive Atlantic Division at 15-6-3 with 33 points - they are still in need of top-six help on the wings. As of now, Fabian Zetterlund, Dylan Cozens, Philip Tomasino, and Drake Batherson occupy the four top-six wing slots.

Aside from Batherson - who has 12 goals and 22 points in 124 games - the other three have combined for a total of 10 goals. In other words, they need proven goal-scorers, and that's something that the Penguins can provide with Rakell and Rust.

Both players have registered seven seasons at 20 or more goals, with all of Rust's coming consecutively in the last seven seasons. Rakell has three 30-goal seasons under his belt, while Rust has just one. But each brings something that Ottawa needs, as Rust's consistency and hard-checking style is a commodity and Rakell's goal-scoring ability and positional versatility - he can man the middle as well - make him valuable.

Each of them play on both special teams units as well, making them even more valuable because the Sens' power play is dead-last in the NHL at a 13.5 percent conversion rate, while the penalty kill is 19th at 76.2 percent. If this team could improve its special teams, it could be even more of a threat in the Eastern Conference.

Of course, there's no guarantee that Staios was specifically in town to see Rakell. It's worth noting that ex-Senator and former captain Erik Karlsson is also employed by the Penguins, and his three goals and 19 points in 22 games this season might make him enticing to his former team in need of a power play quarterback.

But Karlsson has a full-no-movement clause, which is a difficult workaround. And the Senators also don't need as much help on their blue line. So, it's highly likely that Staios was in town to scout Rakell and a few of the Penguins' other forwards such as Tommy Novak or younger guys in Rutger McGroarty, Ville Koivunen, or Avery Hayes.

Regardless, it is more than mildly interesting that the GM of an NHL team with no stakes in the actual game being played travelled all the way to Nashville on Nov. 21 to watch in person. Typically, teams send scouts or even their director of scouting, so this isn't something that happens every day.

So, if you're a Penguins' or a Senators' fan, a potential trade between these two teams could be something to watch in the coming days.

#2833. Walker Kessler flashes ‘f–k yeah’ moment in game of ‘f–k nos’

As Walker Kessler walked into the interview room, he uttered four words.

The Lakers, who have the NBA's best record at 14-3, had eked past the Kings (1-14), who are in the league's cellar.

Walker Kessler (14) of the Los Angeles Lakers shoots a 3-point basket against the Sacramento Kings on Friday, Nov. 20, 2026, in Los Angeles. NBAE via Getty Images

For Kessler, the Lakers' 106-104 win on Friday was mostly forgettable.

He got lit into by Lakers coach JJ Redick at halftime after only having six points and one rebound before turning things around to finish with 15 points, seven rebounds and three blocked shots. He missed two free throws with 5.3 seconds left and the Lakers up, 106-104, a blemish that would've been much worse had Zach LaVine not missed his go-ahead 3-point attempt with 0.9 seconds left.

In a few days, no one is going to remember that the Lakers trailed the Kings by as much as 11 points in a game with 10 lead changes and 15 ties. All that anyone is going to remember is the W.

Kessler has already proven himself as an excellent rim defender and rebounder, but on Friday he revealed that there are some more tricks in his bag.

With 11:42 remaining in the fourth quarter and the Lakers trailing the Kings, 84-82, Collin Sexton passed the ball to the 7-foot-1 center in the corner. Kessler then fired a 24-foot jumper which swished through the net.

So, you know, he's shooting a stunning 100% from beyond the arc.

Dylan Cardwell of the Sacramento Kings plays defense on Walker Kessler of the Los Angeles Lakers in Los Angeles, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

It wasn't exactly the play Redick called. He said it was "unorganized," adding, "I'm not sure why [Kessler] was even in the corner."

"I didn't bat an eye when he shot it," Redick said.

During the preseason, after Redick drew an ATO that involved Kessler, the center wanted to make sure he did his due diligence by inquiring about all possible scenarios.

Walker Kessler grabs a rebound during the NBA Cup game at Crypto.com Arena in Los Angeles, Friday, Nov. 20, 2026. NBAE via Getty Images

"We break the huddle and he's like, 'Coach, what do you want me to do if the ball finds me and we don't get a layup on this play?' Redick recalled Kessler asking. "And I said, 'Shoot it.' And he goes, 'F–k yeah.'"

So in a game with so many "F–k nos," Kessler finally got his moment.

"I feel good," Kessler said, adding a caveat. "Should've made those free throws."

#2831. Indiana Pacers Make Pascal Siakam Announcement Before Knicks Game

The Indiana Pacers could be without their leading scorer when they face the New York Knicks on Sunday, November 22.

All-Star forward Pascal Siakam has been listed as questionable on the NBA's official injury report due to left hip soreness and tightness, leaving his availability uncertain for Indiana's 8 p.m. ET matchup at Madison Square Garden.

The designation adds another concern for a Pacers team already dealing with multiple injuries. Indiana enters Sunday's matchup with a 5-11 record, having lost three consecutive games.

Siakam has been one of the few consistent contributors during Indiana's difficult start. He scored 21 points in Friday's loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers and has missed just one game this season.

As of Saturday evening, Siakam has not been ruled out of Sunday's game against New York. However, his questionable designation means the Pacers have not confirmed that he will play.

Indiana will have another opportunity to update Siakam's status before Sunday's 8 p.m. ET tipoff. His participation in pregame activities and any subsequent injury report updates should provide more clarity.

Siakam's potential absence would be particularly significant given Indiana's existing injury problems.

Four Pacers players have already been ruled out against the Knicks:

Johnny Furphy: Right ACL surgery recovery

Haliburton's absence has been especially damaging. As Heavy previously reported, the star guard is expected to miss at least another two weeks while recovering from his hamstring injury.

That has placed additional responsibility on Siakam, whose scoring and ability to create offense have become increasingly important to Indiana's depleted rotation.

If Siakam cannot play Sunday, the Pacers would be without both of their established All-Star offensive leaders against the defending NBA champions.

Sunday's matchup offers Indiana an opportunity to secure another victory over a familiar Eastern Conference rival.

The Pacers defeated the Knicks 106-104 on November 13, with Siakam leading Indiana with 19 points. Aaron Nesmith added 18 points, while Kelly Oubre Jr. contributed 16.

That victory was one of Indiana's brighter moments this season, but the Pacers have since dropped three consecutive games.

In their latest defeat, Indiana struggled through a disastrous third quarter against Cleveland, getting outscored 43-22 as the Cavaliers pulled away.

The Pacers will now attempt to reverse that slide against a Knicks team that entered Saturday's game against Portland with a 9-6 record.

Indiana's injury situation also places additional importance on contributions from players such as Nesmith, Oubre and center Ivica Zubac.

Those players helped the Pacers beat New York in their previous meeting, but another victory could prove considerably more difficult without Siakam.

The Knicks will be playing the second game of a back-to-back, while Indiana has had a day off since its loss to Cleveland.

Whether Siakam is available to take advantage of that scheduling difference now becomes one of the biggest questions surrounding Sunday's matchup.
